| 4. **Appropriate technology** | Scientifically sound, locally adaptable, acceptable, affordable, maintainable by people | Home-made ORS, MUAC tape, chlorhexidine for cord care |

**Other principles / strategies often added**

| Principle | Example |
|---|---|
| Focus on prevention and promotion | Health education, immunisation |
| Decentralisation | Upazila and union-level planning |
| Use of health manpower (CHWs) | CHCPs, health assistants, FWAs |
| Integration of services | Essential Service Package |
| Referral system | CC → UHC → district hospital |

**Importance**

These principles make care **essential, accessible, acceptable and affordable**, the basis for Health for All and UHC.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Care of the Community).

## DU.46
**Equitable distribution: meaning**

- First principle of PHC: health services must be shared equally by all people irrespective of their ability to pay; all (rich or poor, urban or rural) must have access.
- **Social equity**: equal access for equal need; more resources to those in greater need.

**Why it is essential**

1. Health services were concentrated in cities and hospitals, where a minority lives → **social injustice**.
2. **Inverse care law** (Tudor Hart, 1971): availability of good care varies inversely with need.
3. The poor and rural carry the **highest burden** of disease and death.
4. Health is a **human right**; HFA and UHC are impossible without equity.
5. Inequity wastes resources: costly tertiary care for few, no basic care for many.
6. Equity improves national indicators fastest (IMR, MMR).

**Implementation in Bangladesh**

1. **Community clinics**: one per ~6,000 rural people, within 30 minutes' walk.
2. **Union-level facilities** and **Upazila Health Complexes** in every upazila.
3. **Free or low-cost services**: EPI, essential drugs, FP methods, TB and malaria treatment.
4. **Outreach**: EPI outreach sites, satellite clinics, domiciliary visits by HA and FWA.
5. **Pro-poor schemes**: maternal health voucher scheme; Shasthyo Surokhsha Karmasuchi (health card for the poor).
6. **Urban PHC** for slum dwellers through city corporations and NGOs.
7. **Special areas**: hill tracts, haor, char and coastal areas.
8. **Gender equity**: women-friendly services, female health workers.

**Problems**

Vacant posts in rural facilities, high out-of-pocket spending, urban–rural gap.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Care of the Community).

## DU.47
**Equal vs equitable distribution**

| Point | Equal distribution | Equitable distribution |
|---|---|---|
| Meaning | Same amount of resources to everyone | Resources according to **need** |
| Basis | Sameness | Fairness / justice |
| Assumption | Everyone starts with the same needs | Needs differ between people and areas |
| Effect on gaps | Gaps persist or widen | Gaps narrow |
| Concept | Equality | Equity |
| PHC principle | Not sufficient | **First principle of PHC** |

**Illustrations**

1. **Doctors per upazila**: equal = same number for every upazila; equitable = more staff for remote haor, char and hill upazilas with worse indicators.
2. **Iron-folic acid**: equal = same tablets for all women; equitable = extra supply to pregnant and anaemic women.
3. **Food support**: equal = same ration to every child; equitable = therapeutic food to severely malnourished children.
4. **Health budget**: equal per head ignores need; equitable gives more to districts with high IMR and MMR.
5. **Hospital beds**: equal per city and village is unrealistic; equitable ensures every person can reach basic care (community clinic within 30 minutes' walk).

**Key point**

Equity = equal access for equal need + more for greater need → fair health outcomes.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Care of the Community).

## DU.48
**Definition**

**Appropriate technology**: technology that is scientifically sound, adaptable to local needs, and acceptable to those who apply it and to those for whom it is used, and that can be maintained by the people themselves, using resources the community and country can afford (Park).

**Criteria**

1. Scientifically sound and effective
2. Simple; usable by community health workers or families
3. Low cost, affordable
4. Acceptable (social, cultural)
5. Uses local resources; maintainable by people
6. Safe

**Examples**

| Area | Appropriate technology |
|---|---|
| Diarrhoea | ORS, home-made salt–sugar (lobon-gur) solution, zinc |
| Nutrition | Growth chart, MUAC tape, iodised salt |
| Maternal health | Clean delivery kit, misoprostol for PPH, partograph |
| Newborn care | Kangaroo mother care, chlorhexidine for cord care |
| Water and sanitation | Tube well, chlorination, sanitary pit latrine |
| Immunisation | Cold chain (ice-lined refrigerators, vaccine carriers), VVM |

**Home-made ORS: a classic example**

1. **Scientifically sound**: glucose-coupled sodium absorption replaces water and salt lost in diarrhoea.
2. **Simple**: a pinch of salt, a fistful of gur or sugar, in about half a litre of clean water; any mother can make it.
3. **Low cost**: uses salt, gur and water found in every home.
4. **Acceptable**: familiar ingredients; given by the mother at home.
5. **Available immediately**: no need to wait for packets or a health facility.
6. **Effective**: prevents dehydration deaths; Bangladesh scaled it up nationally through mass teaching of mothers.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Care of the Community).

## DU.49
**Organisation**

Ministry of Health and Family Welfare (MOHFW): two divisions since 2017, Health Services Division; Medical Education and Family Welfare Division.
Directorates: **DGHS** (health services), **DGFP** (family planning), DGME (medical education), DGNM (nursing and midwifery), DGDA (drugs).

**Levels of public health care**

| Level | Facility | Main services |
|---|---|---|
| National / tertiary | Medical college hospitals, specialised institutes | Specialist and super-specialist care, teaching, research |
| District / secondary | District hospital, MCWC | Specialist care, referral |
| Upazila / primary (first referral) | **Upazila Health Complex** (31/50 beds) | Indoor and outdoor care, emergency, EPI, MCH-FP, TB/leprosy, lab, supervision of field staff |
| Union | Union Health and Family Welfare Centre (DGFP), Union Sub-centre / Rural Dispensary (DGHS) | Outpatient care, MCH-FP, normal delivery, immunisation |
| Ward / grass root | **Community clinic** (one per ~6,000 people) | One-stop health, FP, nutrition services; referral |
| Household | HA, FWA (domiciliary) | EPI, FP, health education, surveillance |

Facility count (Bangladesh Health Facility Survey 2017): 420 Upazila Health Complexes; 1,275 union sub-centres; 3,924 UH&FWCs (DGFP); 13,442 community clinics.

**Primary-level flow (upazila to grass root)**

Upazila Health Complex → Union facilities → Community clinic → Household (HA, FWA, CHCP)

**Urban PHC**: Local Government Division (city corporations, municipalities) with NGOs.

**Other sectors**: private hospitals and clinics, NGOs (BRAC, icddr,b), informal providers (village doctors, drug sellers).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Care of the Community); Bangladesh Health Facility Survey 2017 (PMC), https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC8424828/table/T2

## DU.50
**Types of health care services**

**A. By level**

| Level | Description | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Primary | First contact; essential care near home | Community clinic, union centres, UHC outpatient |
| Secondary | Referral care; basic specialists | District hospital |
| Tertiary | Specialised care, teaching, research | Medical college hospitals (e.g., Mugda Medical College Hospital), specialised institutes |

**B. By nature of care**

1. **Promotive**: health education, nutrition counselling.
2. **Preventive**: immunisation, ANC, screening.
3. **Curative**: outpatient, inpatient, emergency, surgery.
4. **Rehabilitative**: physiotherapy, prosthesis, counselling.
5. **Palliative**: pain relief, end-of-life care.

**C. By setting**

Hospital-based (OPD, IPD, emergency, ICU); community-based (outreach, satellite clinics, domiciliary visits); school, workplace and mobile services; telemedicine.

**D. By provider**

Public (MOHFW); private; NGO; informal (village doctors, drug sellers); traditional (Ayurvedic, Unani, homoeopathic).

**E. Special services**

MCH and family planning; school health; occupational health; geriatric care; mental health; diagnostic (lab, imaging); blood transfusion; ambulance and referral.

**Mugda Medical College Hospital (tertiary)**: outpatient, inpatient and emergency care in major specialties, ICU, diagnostic and imaging services, EPI and MCH corner, referral care, teaching and training.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Care of the Community).

## DU.51
**Achievements (since Alma-Ata, 1978)**

| Area | Achievement |
|---|---|
| Child survival | U5MR 146 (1990) → 46 (2014); MDG 4 met; U5MR 33 and IMR 27 per 1000 LB (SVRS 2023) |
| Life expectancy | 72.3 years (SVRS 2023) |
| Fertility | Fall to TFR 2.17 (SVRS 2023) through family planning |
| Immunisation | EPI since 1979; smallpox gone (last case 1975); polio-free (SEAR certified 2014) |
| Disease elimination | Lymphatic filariasis (May 2023) and kala-azar (Oct 2023; first country) eliminated as public health problems (WHO) |
| Diarrhoea | ORS and home-made ORS; zinc scale-up |
| Infrastructure | Community clinics, union centres and UHCs nationwide |
| Workforce | CHCPs, HAs, FWAs, NGO volunteers |
| Partnership | Strong NGO sector (BRAC, icddr,b) |

**Constraints**

1. **Human resources**: vacant posts, absenteeism, few specialists at upazila level.
2. **Maldistribution**: urban concentration of doctors and facilities.
3. **Financing**: low public spending; high **out-of-pocket** payment.
4. **Weak referral** and frequent self-referral.
5. **Quality gaps**: drugs, equipment, laboratory support.
6. **Urban PHC** weak, especially for slum dwellers.
7. **Double burden**: rising NCDs with remaining infections and malnutrition.
8. **Coverage gaps**: measles-rubella first dose fell to 86% (CES 2023) → large measles outbreak in 2026.
9. Weak intersectoral coordination and community participation in some areas.
10. Unregulated private sector and informal providers.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Care of the Community); BBS SVRS 2023 (The Financial Express, 2024), https://today.thefinancialexpress.com.bd/print/life-expectancy-falls-in-bd-bbs-1724781053; WHO, 2023, https://www.who.int/news/item/31-10-2023-bangladesh-achieves-historic-milestone-by-eliminating-kala-azar-as-a-public-health-problem

## DU.52
**Community clinic (CC)**

- Ward-level, first-contact government facility; one per ~6,000 rural people within 30 minutes' walk.
- **One-stop** outlet for health, family planning and nutrition.
- Run by a **Community Health Care Provider (CHCP)**, supported by the Health Assistant and Family Welfare Assistant; managed with the community (Community Group).

**Services**

1. **Maternal care**: ANC, PNC, danger-sign identification, iron-folic acid; normal delivery in selected CCs.
2. **Family planning**: pills, condoms, injectables; counselling.
3. **Child health**: IMCI, growth monitoring, IYCF counselling.
4. **Immunisation**: EPI outreach site; vitamin A campaigns.
5. **Nutrition**: counselling, MUAC screening, micronutrient supplements.
6. **Treatment** of minor ailments and first aid; free essential drugs.
7. **NCD screening**: blood pressure, diabetes.
8. **Health education**: hygiene, sanitation, safe water, adolescent health.
9. **Surveillance**: reporting of births, deaths, notifiable diseases.
10. **Referral** of complicated cases to union, UHC and district hospital.
11. **Record keeping** and online reporting.

**Role at grass-root level**

1. Brings PHC to the **doorstep** of rural people.
2. Reduces distance, cost and delay.
3. Serves mainly **women and children**.
4. Model of **community participation** (donated land, community groups).
5. Strengthens **referral** and continuity of care.
6. Supports national programmes (EPI, FP, nutrition, TB) and progress toward UHC.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Care of the Community); Daily Observer (bdnews24), 2025, https://www.observerbd.com/news/540274

## DU.53
**Upazila Health and Family Planning Officer (UH&FPO): community physician at upazila level**

**A. Administrative**

1. Head of the **Upazila Health Complex** and all upazila health services.
2. Supervises doctors, nurses, SACMOs, HIs, AHIs, HAs, CHCPs and community clinics.
3. Planning, budgeting, logistics, drugs and equipment management.
4. Coordination with upazila parishad, UNO, Upazila Family Planning Officer, education, DPHE and NGOs (**intersectoral**).
5. Chairs or joins upazila health committees.

**B. Public health / preventive**

1. Implements national programmes: EPI, TB, leprosy, malaria, dengue, nutrition, NCD, MNCH, school health.
2. **Disease surveillance** and notification; outbreak investigation and control.
3. Health education and community mobilisation.
4. Environmental sanitation and safe water promotion (with DPHE).
5. Disaster preparedness and emergency response.

**C. Clinical**

1. Ensures outpatient, inpatient, emergency and referral services.
2. Quality of care, infection prevention, rational drug use.

**D. Information and evaluation**

1. Collection and reporting of health data (HMIS/DHIS2); monthly reports to the civil surgeon.
2. Monitoring, supervision and evaluation of services.

**E. Training and research**

Training of staff; supervision of interns; operational research.

**F. Legal**

Medicolegal duties (injury and post-mortem reports where applicable), birth and death certificates, enforcement of public health laws.

**Five main duties (short answer)**

Administration of UHC; implementation of national programmes; surveillance and epidemic control; supervision of field staff and community clinics; health information and reporting.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Care of the Community; Health Planning and Management).

## DU.54
**PHC elements in diarrhoeal disease control**

| PHC element | Role in diarrhoea |
|---|---|
| Health education | Hand-washing with soap, food hygiene, safe disposal of child faeces, home-made ORS, danger signs |
| Food and nutrition | Exclusive breastfeeding to 6 months, complementary feeding, continued feeding during diarrhoea, vitamin A |
| Safe water and sanitation | Tube wells, water treatment, sanitary latrines, ending open defecation |
| MCH | IMCI; care of young children; growth monitoring |
| Immunisation | Measles vaccine (reduces post-measles diarrhoea); oral cholera vaccine in outbreaks and camps |
| Local endemic disease control | Cholera surveillance, outbreak response |
| Treatment of common diseases | **ORS + zinc** + continued feeding; antibiotics only for dysentery and cholera; IV fluids for severe dehydration |
| Essential drugs | ORS packets, zinc tablets at community clinics and UHCs |

**Prevention, control and treatment**

1. **Prevention**: water, sanitation, hygiene (WASH), breastfeeding, nutrition, immunisation.
2. **Control**: surveillance, early detection of outbreaks, chlorination, health education.
3. **Treatment**: case management at home (ORS), community clinics (IMCI), UHC and hospitals (severe cases).

**Principles applied**

Community participation (mothers making ORS); appropriate technology (ORS, zinc); intersectoral coordination (DPHE, local government); equitable distribution (free ORS).

**Bangladesh**

ORS pioneered by icddr,b; by 2014 about 80% of children with diarrhoea received ORS and 34% ORS + zinc.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Epidemiology of Communicable Diseases; Health Care of the Community); J Glob Health, 2019, https://jogha.org/documents/issue201901/jogh-09-010503.htm

## DU.55
**Community clinic: excellent example of community participation**

**1. Planning and establishment**
- Built on **land donated** by the local community.
- Site chosen with local people for easy access.

**2. Management**
- **Community Group (CG)**: local representatives (union parishad member, teachers, women, donors) manage the clinic, monitor attendance, solve problems.
- Three **Community Support Groups (CSGs)**: create awareness, mobilise people, help referral, raise local funds.

**3. Resource contribution**
- Local donations (money, materials, labour) for repairs, furniture, water, electricity.
- Volunteers support campaigns.

**4. Use and demand**
- Community mobilises women, children and the poor to use services.
- Feedback improves service quality and timing.

**5. Accountability**
- Community monitors staff presence and drug supply.

**6. Partnership model**
- Government provides building, staff (CHCP), drugs, logistics; community provides land and management support → **public–community partnership**.

**Why it fits the PHC principle**

| Feature of community participation | Community clinic |
|---|---|
| Involvement in planning | Site selection, land donation |
| Involvement in implementation | CG and CSGs |
| Involvement in maintenance | Local funds and repairs |
| Use of local resources | Land, money, volunteers |
| Self-reliance | Community "owns" the clinic |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Care of the Community).

## DU.56
**Health team**

A group of persons with different skills who share a common health goal, each contributing according to competence, working in coordination (doctor, nurse, health assistant, FWA, CHCP, SACMO, health inspector, lab technician, pharmacist, community volunteers).

**Importance**

1. **Comprehensive care**: no single person has all skills for promotive, preventive, curative and rehabilitative care.
2. **Coverage**: reaches large populations, homes and remote areas.
3. **Efficient use of manpower**: tasks delegated to the right level (task-sharing); doctor's time saved for complex cases.
4. **Cost-effective**: lower-cost workers deliver routine services.
5. **Continuity of care**: from home to community clinic to hospital, through referral.
6. **Implements national programmes**: EPI, FP, TB, malaria, nutrition, NCDs.
7. **Community participation**: field workers link people with health services.
8. **Better quality and safety**: mutual support, supervision, shared decisions.
9. **Intersectoral action**: includes teachers, sanitary workers, social workers.
10. **Training and supervision**: senior members guide juniors.
11. **Data and surveillance**: field staff collect information for planning.

**Requirements for an effective team**

Common goal; clear roles; good leadership; communication; mutual respect; supervision; training; adequate resources.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Care of the Community).

## DU.57
**Community health workers (CHWs)**

Health workers chosen from and working in their own communities, with short training, providing promotive, preventive and basic curative care and linking people with the health system.
Bangladesh: Health Assistants, Family Welfare Assistants, CHCPs, NGO volunteers (e.g., BRAC *shasthya shebika*).

**Justification**

1. **Reach**: deliver care to homes in remote, poor and hard-to-reach areas → **equity**.
2. **First contact**: implement PHC at the grass root.
3. **Trust and acceptability**: same language, culture and community.
4. **Cost-effective**: low cost, high coverage; free scarce doctors for complex care.
5. **Proven impact**: ORS, family planning, EPI, vitamin A, TB DOTS, malaria and kala-azar control.
6. **Health education and behaviour change** in families.
7. **Surveillance**: early reporting of outbreaks, births, deaths.
8. **Community participation**: mobilise people and resources.
9. **Referral**: identify danger signs and refer early.
10. **Resilience**: maintain services during disasters and epidemics.
11. **UHC**: essential for reaching everyone with essential services (WHO guideline on CHW programmes, 2018).

**Conditions for success**

Proper selection, training, supervision, supplies, fair pay, career path, integration into the health system.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Care of the Community); WHO guideline on health policy and system support to optimize CHW programmes, 2018.

## DU.58
**Why "complex"**

The Upazila Health Complex is not just a hospital; it is a **combination (complex) of many health facilities, services and offices in one campus** serving the whole upazila.

**Components**

1. **Hospital**: indoor (31/50 beds), outpatient, emergency, operation theatre, labour room.
2. **Diagnostic services**: laboratory, X-ray, ECG.
3. **Preventive and public health units**: EPI, TB and leprosy, malaria, nutrition, surveillance.
4. **MCH and family planning** services (with the Upazila Family Planning Office).
5. **Dental, eye and other units** where staffed.
6. **Administrative office** of the UH&FPO; stores; records and HMIS.
7. **Training** facilities for staff and community workers.
8. **Residential quarters** for doctors, nurses and staff.
9. **Supervisory base** for field staff (HI, AHI, HA) and community clinics.
10. **Ambulance and referral** services.

**Functions combined**

Curative + preventive + promotive + rehabilitative + administrative + training + supervision → **integrated, comprehensive care**.

Hence it is a "complex" rather than a single hospital.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Care of the Community).

## DU.59
**Health Assistant (HA)**

Male or female domiciliary field worker of DGHS at **ward level**; supervised by the Assistant Health Inspector → Health Inspector → UH&FPO.

**Roles in the community**

1. **Immunisation (EPI)**: registers eligible children and women; vaccinates at outreach sites; tracks defaulters.
2. **Surveillance**: reports AFP, measles, neonatal tetanus, diarrhoea outbreaks and other notifiable diseases.
3. **Health education**: hygiene, safe water, sanitation, breastfeeding, nutrition, danger signs.
4. **Communicable disease control**: TB and leprosy case finding and treatment follow-up; malaria and kala-azar activities in endemic areas; dengue source reduction.
5. **Vitamin A, deworming** and other campaigns.
6. **MCH**: identifies pregnant women, encourages ANC and facility delivery.
7. **School health**: health education and screening in schools.
8. **Registration**: births, deaths, pregnancies; household records.
9. **Community clinic support**: works at the CC on scheduled days.
10. **Referral** of sick persons to CC, union centre or UHC.
11. **Disaster and epidemic response**: chlorination, ORS distribution.

**Importance**

Main link between households and the health system; essential for EPI success and early outbreak detection.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Care of the Community).

## DU.60
**Answer**

**Upazila Health Complex (UHC)** is the grass-root level (first-level) **hospital** of Bangladesh.

**Reasons**

1. Lowest level with **indoor (inpatient) beds** (31/50 beds) in every upazila.
2. **First referral** level for community clinics and union facilities.
3. Provides emergency care, normal and assisted delivery, minor surgery, laboratory and X-ray.
4. Integrates curative, preventive and family planning services.

**Note**

- The **community clinic** (ward level, one per ~6,000 people) is the grass-root **health facility**, but it is outpatient only, not a hospital.
- Union facilities (UH&FWC, union sub-centre) are mainly outpatient; a few have 10–20 beds.

**Flow**

Community clinic → Union facility → **Upazila Health Complex** → District hospital → Medical college hospital

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Care of the Community).

## DU.61
**What it is**

**Health Survey and Development Committee** (India), 1943–1946, chaired by **Sir Joseph Bhore**; reviewed health conditions of undivided British India (including present Bangladesh) and planned future health services. Report: 1946.

**Concept**

Health services should be **comprehensive**, **integrated** (preventive + curative) and reach the **rural** population through health centres; doctors should be **social physicians**.

**Objectives**

1. Survey the existing health situation and organisation.
2. Recommend future development of health services.

**Recommendations**

1. **Integration of preventive and curative services** at all administrative levels.
2. **Primary health centres** in two stages:
   - **Short-term**: one PHC per 40,000 population, with 2 doctors, 1 nurse, 4 public health nurses, 4 midwives, 4 trained dais, 2 sanitary inspectors, 2 health assistants, 1 pharmacist and 15 class IV staff; secondary health centres to support and supervise PHCs.
   - **Long-term ("3 million plan")**: primary health units with 75-bed hospitals for 10,000–20,000 people; secondary units with 650-bed hospitals; district hospitals with 2,500 beds.
3. **Medical education**: 3 months' training in preventive and social medicine to prepare "**social physicians**".

**Significance**

Basis of rural health centres and PHC in the subcontinent; precursor of thana (upazila) health complexes; introduced the idea of comprehensive health care.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Health Care of the Community).

## DU.62
**Diseases of social origin**

Diseases whose main causes are social conditions (poverty, illiteracy, overcrowding, unemployment, customs, lifestyle) rather than biology alone.

| Social factor | Diseases |
|---|---|
| Poverty, overcrowding, malnutrition | Tuberculosis, leprosy, protein-energy malnutrition, anaemia |
| Poor sanitation and unsafe water | Cholera, typhoid, diarrhoea, hepatitis A and E, worm infestations |
| Sexual behaviour, migration | STIs, HIV/AIDS |
| Substance use | Alcoholism, drug addiction, tobacco-related cancers and COPD |
| Urban lifestyle, affluence | Obesity, diabetes, hypertension, coronary heart disease |
| Social stress, family breakdown | Depression, anxiety, suicide, juvenile delinquency |
| Industrialisation, traffic | Occupational diseases, road traffic accidents |
| Customs and gender inequality | Child marriage → maternal deaths; female malnutrition; domestic violence |
| Neglect and violence | Child abuse, battered baby syndrome |

**Features**

1. Social causes → social remedies (education, employment, housing, laws).
2. Mostly **preventable**.
3. Often cluster in the poor.
4. Studied by **social medicine**.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Man and Medicine; Medicine and Social Sciences).

## DU.63
**Meaning**

**Natural history of disease**: the way a disease evolves over time in a person, from the earliest stage of its pre-pathogenesis to its termination as recovery, disability or death, **without any treatment or intervention**.

**Phases**

| Phase | Features |
|---|---|
| **1. Pre-pathogenesis** | Before man is involved; disease agent present in the environment; interaction of **agent, host and environment** creates the stimulus; person is "at risk" |
| **2. Pathogenesis** | Begins with entry of the agent into the host; agent multiplies, tissue changes occur |

**Stages of pathogenesis**

Stimulus → Early pathogenesis (incubation / pre-symptomatic) → Early discernible lesions → Advanced disease → Convalescence → Outcome (recovery, disability, defect, chronic state, death)

- **Clinical horizon**: line separating subclinical from clinical disease.
- Infectious diseases: incubation period; chronic diseases: latent period.

**Levels of prevention along natural history (Leavell and Clark)**

| Stage | Level | Mode of intervention |
|---|---|---|
| Pre-pathogenesis | Primary prevention | Health promotion; specific protection |
| Early pathogenesis | Secondary prevention | Early diagnosis and treatment |
| Late pathogenesis | Tertiary prevention | Disability limitation; rehabilitation |

**Implications / uses**

1. Basis of the **levels of prevention**.
2. Helps plan **screening** (detect early, pre-symptomatic disease).
3. Shows where the **iceberg** (hidden disease) lies.
4. Natural history of chronic diseases is best studied by **cohort studies**.
5. Helps predict prognosis and evaluate interventions.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Concept of Health and Disease).

## DU.64
**Definition**

**Miasmatic theory**: the belief that disease is caused by **"miasma"** (bad air), foul-smelling, poisonous vapours rising from decaying organic matter, filth, sewage, marshes and swamps.

**Features**

1. Prevailed from ancient times to the late 19th century (before the germ theory).
2. Disease attributed to environment and smell, not to specific organisms.
3. Name **malaria** comes from Italian *mal aria*, "bad air".
4. Cholera, plague and typhus were blamed on miasma.

**Contribution**

1. Led to the **sanitary movement**: cleaning streets, drainage, sewage disposal, safe water (Chadwick, Public Health Act 1848; Florence Nightingale).
2. These measures reduced disease even though the theory was wrong.

**Decline**

Disproved by John Snow (cholera via water, 1854) and the **germ theory** (Pasteur, Koch).

**Theories of disease causation (sequence)**

Supernatural → Theory of humours → **Miasmatic** → Germ theory → Epidemiological triad → Multifactorial causation (web of causation)

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Concept of Health and Disease).

## DU.65
**Multifactorial causation**

- Disease results from the **interaction of many factors** (agent, host, environment, lifestyle), not a single cause.
- Early proponent: **Pettenkofer** of Munich (19th century).
- **Web of causation** (MacMahon and Pugh): a chain of interlinked factors; removing some links can prevent disease.
- Especially relevant to **non-communicable diseases**; also explains infections (agent is necessary but not sufficient).

**Examples**

| Disease | Factors |
|---|---|
| Coronary heart disease | Hyperlipidaemia, smoking, hypertension, diabetes, obesity, physical inactivity, stress, family history |
| Tuberculosis | *M. tuberculosis* + poverty, malnutrition, overcrowding, HIV, diabetes, smoking |
| Road traffic accident | Driver (alcohol, fatigue), vehicle, road, weather, law enforcement |
| Lung cancer | Smoking, air pollution, occupational exposures, genetic factors |

**Implications**

Prevention can act on any modifiable factor; multiple interventions together give best results.

**Self-care**

- **Definition (WHO, 2019)**: the ability of individuals, families and communities to promote health, prevent disease, maintain health, and cope with illness and disability with or without the support of a health worker.
- Park: activities people undertake to promote their own health, prevent disease, limit illness and restore health, using their own knowledge and skills.

**Examples**

1. Observing symptoms and signs (fever, lump in breast).
2. Self-medication for minor illness (paracetamol, ORS).
3. Self-monitoring: blood pressure, blood glucose, weight.
4. Self-tests: pregnancy test, HIV self-test.
5. Dressing minor wounds; first aid.
6. Healthy lifestyle: diet, exercise, no tobacco.
7. Adherence to treatment in chronic diseases (insulin self-injection).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Concept of Health and Disease).

## DU.66
**Epidemiological (ecological) triad**

Disease results from interaction of three components:

Agent ↔ Host ↔ Environment

| Component | Meaning | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| **Agent** | Factor whose presence (or absence) is needed for disease | Biological (bacteria, viruses), nutrient, physical, chemical, mechanical, social agents |
| **Host** | Human (or animal) who harbours the disease | Age, sex, genetics, immunity, nutrition, behaviour, occupation |
| **Environment** | All external conditions influencing agent and host | Physical (climate, water), biological (vectors, animals), psychosocial (poverty, customs) |

**Role in disease causation**

1. Health = **equilibrium** among agent, host and environment.
2. Disease = **imbalance**: more virulent agent, more susceptible host, or environment favouring agent.
3. Environment links agent and host (e.g., stagnant water → mosquitoes → malaria).
4. Model suits **communicable diseases**; for NCDs the **web of causation** is preferred.
5. Prevention can act on any arm: kill agent, protect host, modify environment.

**Example: cholera**

Agent: *Vibrio cholerae* | Host: low gastric acidity, malnutrition, blood group O | Environment: contaminated water, poor sanitation, floods.

**Modes of transmission**

| Direct | Indirect |
|---|---|
| Direct contact (STIs, skin infections) | Vehicle-borne (water, food, blood) |
| Droplet infection (measles, influenza) | Vector-borne: mechanical, biological (propagative, cyclo-propagative, cyclo-developmental) |
| Contact with soil (tetanus, hookworm) | Airborne (droplet nuclei, dust) |
| Inoculation into skin or mucosa (rabies) | Fomite-borne (towels, utensils) |
| Transplacental (rubella, HIV, syphilis) | Unclean hands and fingers |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Concept of Health and Disease; Principles of Epidemiology).

## DU.67
**Definition**

**Disease agent**: a substance, living or non-living, or a force, tangible or intangible, whose excessive presence or relative lack may initiate or perpetuate a disease process.

**Classification of agents (Park)**

| Agent | Examples |
|---|---|
| 1. **Biological** | Viruses (measles), rickettsiae (typhus), bacteria (TB), fungi (candida), protozoa (malaria), metazoa (hookworm) |
| 2. **Nutrient** | Deficiency or excess of protein, fat, carbohydrate, vitamins, minerals, water (kwashiorkor, rickets, obesity) |
| 3. **Physical** | Heat, cold, humidity, pressure, radiation, electricity, sound (heat stroke, deafness) |
| 4. **Chemical: endogenous** | Accumulated urea (uraemia), bilirubin (jaundice), ketones, uric acid (gout), calcium carbonate (stones) |
| 4. **Chemical: exogenous** | Allergens, metals (arsenic, lead), fumes, dust, gases, pesticides |
| 5. **Mechanical** | Chronic friction, injuries, fractures, sprains, accidents |
| 6. **Absence or insufficiency or excess of a factor necessary to health** | Hormones (insulin → diabetes), enzymes, chromosomal factors (Down syndrome), immunological factors (agammaglobulinaemia), structure (congenital heart defect) |
| 7. **Social** | Poverty, smoking, alcohol and drug abuse, unhealthy lifestyle, social isolation, maternal deprivation |

**Responsible agent vs sufficient cause**

An agent is usually **necessary but not sufficient**; host and environment decide whether disease develops.

**Clinical vs community medicine (brief)**

Clinical medicine finds the agent in a patient and treats it; community medicine studies agents, host and environment in populations and controls their spread.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Concept of Health and Disease).

## DU.68
**Definition**

**Risk factor**: an attribute or exposure that is significantly associated with the development of a disease (Park). Its presence increases the probability of disease; its removal reduces it.

**Features**

1. Shows **association**, not always proof of cause.
2. Detected by **epidemiological studies** (case-control, cohort).
3. Usually several risk factors act together (multifactorial causation).
4. Many are **modifiable** → basis of prevention.

**Types**

| Type | Examples |
|---|---|
| Modifiable | Smoking, high BP, high cholesterol, obesity, physical inactivity, unhealthy diet, alcohol |
| Non-modifiable | Age, sex, family history, genetic make-up, ethnicity |

**Related terms**

- **Risk**: probability that an event (disease) will occur.
- **Risk marker / indicator**: attribute linked with increased risk but not causal (e.g., low social class).
- **Risk group**: group of people at higher risk (e.g., pregnant women, under-fives, elderly, health workers for hepatitis B).
- **Risk factor vs cause**: a cause is shown to produce disease; a risk factor may be causal or only associated.

**Uses**

1. Predict who will get disease (**risk prediction**).
2. Target **high-risk** groups for screening and prevention.
3. **Primordial and primary prevention** (tobacco control, salt reduction).
4. Plan health services and education.

**Examples by disease**

CHD: smoking, hypertension, hyperlipidaemia | Cervical cancer: HPV, early marriage, multiparity | TB: HIV, malnutrition, diabetes.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Concept of Health and Disease).

## DU.69
**Concept**

The **disease cycle (chain of infection)**: the sequence of links by which an infectious agent leaves one host and infects another. Infection continues only if every link is present; **breaking any link stops transmission**.

**Six stages (links)**

Infectious agent → Reservoir / source → Portal of exit → Mode of transmission → Portal of entry → Susceptible host → (back to agent/reservoir)

| Stage | Meaning | Example (TB) | Control measure |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1. Infectious agent | Organism causing disease | *M. tuberculosis* | Early diagnosis, treatment (DOTS) |
| 2. Reservoir / source | Where the agent lives and multiplies | Sputum-positive patient | Case finding, isolation, treatment |
| 3. Portal of exit | Route by which agent leaves | Respiratory tract (cough) | Cough etiquette, masks |
| 4. Mode of transmission | How agent reaches new host | Droplet nuclei (airborne) | Ventilation, sunlight, UV |
| 5. Portal of entry | Route into new host | Respiratory tract | Masks (N95) for health workers |
| 6. Susceptible host | Person who can be infected | Malnourished, HIV-positive | BCG, nutrition, TB preventive therapy |

**Uses**

1. Explains how infection spreads.
2. Identifies the **weakest link** to target control.
3. Guides **infection prevention** in hospitals (hand hygiene, sterilisation, isolation).
4. Basis of the three control approaches: **control of reservoir, interruption of transmission, protection of susceptible**.
5. Helps outbreak investigation and health education.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Principles of Epidemiology; Concept of Health and Disease).

## DU.70
**Iceberg of disease**

A concept comparing disease in a community to an iceberg:

| Part | Represents |
|---|---|
| **Floating tip** (visible) | Clinical cases seen by doctors |
| **Waterline** | Line between apparent and inapparent disease |
| **Submerged mass** (hidden, larger) | Undiagnosed cases, subclinical (inapparent) infections, carriers, latent and pre-symptomatic disease |

**Examples**

Hypertension, diabetes, anaemia, malnutrition, mental illness, TB, polio, hepatitis B; hidden cases far outnumber diagnosed ones.
Iceberg is absent or small in rabies, measles and tetanus (almost all infections are clinical).

**Epidemiological importance**

1. Clinicians see only the tip; the true **burden** is much larger.
2. The hidden part is a major **source of infection** (carriers, subclinical cases) → spread continues unnoticed.
3. Justifies **screening** and **active case finding** to detect hidden cases early (secondary prevention).
4. Hospital data alone **underestimate** disease; community surveys are needed (**community diagnosis**).
5. Guides **planning and resource allocation**.
6. Explains why disease **control is difficult** for diseases with large submerged parts.
7. Underlines community medicine's focus on the **whole population**, not only patients.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Concept of Health and Disease).

## DU.71
**Definition**

**Opportunistic infection**: infection by an organism that usually does not cause disease (or causes only mild disease) in a healthy person, but causes disease when the host's defences are weakened, i.e., it takes the "opportunity" of lowered immunity.

**Conditions that lower immunity**

HIV/AIDS; cancer chemotherapy; organ transplant (immunosuppressants); long-term steroids; diabetes; severe malnutrition; extremes of age; congenital immunodeficiency.

**Examples**

| Group | Organism / disease |
|---|---|
| Bacterial | Tuberculosis, *Mycobacterium avium* complex, recurrent bacterial pneumonia |
| Fungal | Oral and oesophageal candidiasis, *Pneumocystis jirovecii* pneumonia, cryptococcal meningitis, histoplasmosis |
| Viral | CMV retinitis, herpes zoster, HSV ulcers, HHV-8 (Kaposi's sarcoma) |
| Protozoal | Cerebral toxoplasmosis, cryptosporidiosis |

**Importance**

1. Main cause of illness and death in **AIDS**; some are AIDS-defining.
2. **TB** is the commonest opportunistic infection in people with HIV in South Asia.

**Prevention**

Antiretroviral therapy; cotrimoxazole prophylaxis; TB preventive therapy; vaccination; good nutrition; infection control in hospitals.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Epidemiology of Communicable Diseases).

## DU.72
**Host factors (intrinsic factors)**

| Group | Factors | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| 1. **Demographic** | Age, sex, ethnicity | Measles in children; breast cancer in women; thalassaemia in certain groups |
| 2. **Biological** | Genetic factors; biochemical levels (cholesterol); blood groups; physiological state (pregnancy, puberty, stress, fatigue); immunity and previous disease; nutrition | Sickle-cell trait protects against malaria; blood group O and cholera; pregnancy and anaemia |
| 3. **Social and economic** | Socio-economic status, education, occupation, stress, marital status, housing | Silicosis in stone crushers; TB with poverty and overcrowding |
| 4. **Lifestyle** | Personality traits, living habits, nutrition, physical exercise, use of alcohol, drugs, smoking, behaviour | CHD with smoking and inactivity; HIV with risky sexual behaviour |

**Other host factors**

- **Immune status**: vaccination, natural immunity.
- **Personal hygiene**: scabies, worm infestation.
- **Psychological factors**: personality type A and CHD.

**Importance**

Host factors decide **susceptibility**; they explain why only some exposed persons fall ill and help identify **high-risk groups** for prevention.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Concept of Health and Disease).

## DU.73
**Definitions**

1. **Webster**: a condition in which body health is impaired; a departure from a state of health; an alteration of the body that interrupts performance of vital functions.
2. **Oxford English Dictionary**: a condition of the body, or of some part or organ, in which its functions are disturbed or deranged.
3. **Ecological view**: maladjustment of the human organism to its environment.

**Disease, illness, sickness**

| Term | Meaning | Perspective |
|---|---|---|
| **Disease** | Physiological or psychological dysfunction | Doctor's (objective) |
| **Illness** | Subjective state of feeling unwell | Patient's (subjective) |
| **Sickness** | State of social dysfunction; role the person takes when ill ("sick role") | Society's |

A person may have disease without illness (hypertension) or illness without disease (anxiety).

**Features**

- Has a cause (agent, host, environment interaction).
- Follows a **natural history**.
- Has a spectrum: inapparent → mild → severe → fatal.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Concept of Health and Disease).

## DU.74
**Definition**

**Disease control**: ongoing operations aimed at reducing:
1. the **incidence** of disease,
2. the **duration** of disease and so the risk of transmission,
3. the **effects** of infection, including physical and psychosocial complications,
4. the **financial burden** to the community.

The aim is to reduce the disease to a level where it is **no longer a public health problem**; the agent continues to exist in the community.

**Spectrum**

Control → Elimination → Eradication

**Measures**

| Approach | Examples |
|---|---|
| Control of reservoir | Early diagnosis, treatment, isolation, quarantine, notification |
| Interruption of transmission | Water purification, sanitation, vector control, hand hygiene, safe sex |
| Protection of susceptible | Immunisation, chemoprophylaxis, nutrition, personal protection |
| Surveillance | Notification, case detection, outbreak response, monitoring of trends |

Supported by health education, intersectoral action and community participation.

**Examples (Bangladesh)**

TB (DOTS), malaria (bed nets, rapid tests), dengue (source reduction, case management), diarrhoea (ORS, WASH).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Concept of Health and Disease).

## DU.75
**Levels of prevention and modes of intervention**

| Level | Phase of natural history | Mode of intervention | Examples |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Primordial** | Before risk factors appear | Prevent emergence of risk factors (individual and mass education, policy) | Discourage children from smoking; promote healthy diet and play; tobacco laws |
| **Primary** | Pre-pathogenesis | 1. **Health promotion** | Health education, nutrition, sanitation, housing, lifestyle change |
| | | 2. **Specific protection** | Immunisation, chemoprophylaxis, iodised salt, vitamin A, helmets, protective gear, condom use |
| **Secondary** | Early pathogenesis | 3. **Early diagnosis and treatment** | Screening (BP, diabetes, VIA), case finding (TB), treatment of malaria, IMCI |
| **Tertiary** | Late pathogenesis | 4. **Disability limitation** | Good diabetes control to prevent amputation; treatment of stroke; leprosy MDT and care |
| | | 5. **Rehabilitation** | Medical, vocational, social, psychological: physiotherapy, artificial limb, job training |

**Key points**

1. Levels follow the **natural history of disease** (Leavell and Clark).
2. Five modes of intervention: health promotion, specific protection, early diagnosis and treatment, disability limitation, rehabilitation.
3. Earlier prevention is cheaper and more effective.
4. Secondary prevention is often the main option for diseases without primary prevention.

**Example: diabetes at all levels**

Primordial: healthy school food | Primary: weight control, exercise | Secondary: blood glucose screening | Tertiary: foot care, eye laser, rehabilitation after amputation.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Concept of Health and Disease).

## DU.76
**Definition**

**Primordial prevention**: prevention of the emergence or development of **risk factors** in countries or population groups in which they have not yet appeared.

**Features**

1. Newest level; acts **before** risk factors appear.
2. Especially for **chronic NCDs** (CHD, hypertension, diabetes, cancers).
3. Targets **children and young people**, before harmful habits form.
4. Works mainly through **individual and mass education** and healthy public policy.
5. Population-wide; not disease-specific.

**Examples**

| Risk factor prevented | Measure |
|---|---|
| Smoking | Tobacco control law, school education, ban on advertising, taxes |
| Unhealthy diet | School nutrition, limits on trans fat, salt and sugar; healthy food labelling |
| Physical inactivity | Playgrounds, parks, safe walking and cycling paths |
| Obesity | Breastfeeding promotion, healthy lifestyle education |
| Alcohol and drugs | Laws, awareness, family support |
| Air pollution | Clean fuel, emission control |

**Primordial vs primary prevention**

| Primordial | Primary |
|---|---|
| Prevents risk factors | Prevents disease in those with/without risk factors |
| Before risk factors appear | Pre-pathogenesis phase |
| Mainly policy and education | Health promotion + specific protection |

**Bangladesh examples**

Smoking and Tobacco Products Usage (Control) Act 2005 (amended 2013); school health education; anti-tobacco campaigns.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Concept of Health and Disease).

## DU.77
**Definition**

**Primary prevention**: action taken **before the onset of disease** that removes the possibility that the disease will ever occur; intervention in the **pre-pathogenesis** phase.

**Modes of intervention**

**1. Health promotion** (not disease-specific)
- Health education
- Environmental modifications (safe water, sanitation, housing)
- Nutritional interventions (food fortification, supplementation)
- Lifestyle and behavioural changes

**2. Specific protection** (disease-specific)
- Immunisation (EPI: BCG, pentavalent, MR, PCV)
- Use of specific nutrients (iodised salt, vitamin A, iron-folic acid)
- Chemoprophylaxis (antimalarials for travellers, cotrimoxazole in HIV)
- Protection against occupational hazards (masks, earplugs)
- Protection against accidents (helmets, seat belts)
- Protection from carcinogens (HPV vaccine, no tobacco)
- Avoidance of allergens
- Control of specific environmental hazards (air pollution)
- Quality control of food, drugs and cosmetics

**Approaches**

| Approach | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Population (mass) strategy** | Lower the risk factor level in the whole population | Salt reduction for all |
| **High-risk strategy** | Target persons at special risk | Statins and counselling for high-risk adults |

**Importance**

Most effective and cheapest level; "prevention is better than cure"; WHO and Alma-Ata stress it.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Concept of Health and Disease).

## DU.78
**Definitions**

| Term | Meaning | Measures needed after achievement | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Control** | Reduction of incidence, prevalence, morbidity or mortality to an acceptable level | Continued | TB, malaria |
| **Elimination as a public health problem** (WHO) | Achievement of defined measurable targets (e.g., below a threshold rate) in an area | Continued | Kala-azar: <1 case/10,000 at upazila level |
| **Elimination of disease** | Reduction to **zero incidence** of disease in a defined geographical area | Continued | Neonatal tetanus in a country |
| **Elimination of infection** | Reduction to **zero incidence of infection** in a defined area | Continued | Polio in a region |
| **Eradication** | Permanent reduction to **zero worldwide incidence of infection**; termination of all transmission by extermination of the agent | **Not needed** | Smallpox |
| **Extinction** | Agent no longer exists in nature or laboratory | None | None yet |

**Eradication: smallpox**

- Last natural case worldwide: Somalia, 1977; Bangladesh's last case: 1975.
- WHO declared global eradication in **1980**.
- Candidates: poliomyelitis, dracunculiasis (guinea worm), yaws.

**Elimination: Bangladesh**

1. **Lymphatic filariasis**: eliminated as a public health problem (WHO validated, May 2023).
2. **Kala-azar**: eliminated as a public health problem (WHO validated, October 2023); first country in the world.
3. **Polio**: free, with SEAR certified polio-free in 2014.

**Key difference**

Elimination = regional, control measures must continue; eradication = global, measures can stop.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Concept of Health and Disease); WHO, 2023, https://www.who.int/news/item/31-10-2023-bangladesh-achieves-historic-milestone-by-eliminating-kala-azar-as-a-public-health-problem

## DU.79
**Definitions**

1. **Park**: the continuous scrutiny of the factors that determine the occurrence and distribution of disease and other conditions of ill health.
2. **CDC / WHO (public health surveillance)**: the ongoing, systematic collection, analysis and interpretation of health-related data essential to planning, implementation and evaluation of public health practice, with timely dissemination to those who need to know, linked to action.

Short form: **"information for action"**.

**Objectives**

1. Provide information on new and changing trends of disease.
2. Detect outbreaks early and start control.
3. Monitor progress of control programmes.
4. Evaluate interventions.
5. Help planning and resource allocation.

**Types**

| Type | Example |
|---|---|
| Passive | Routine reports from health facilities (HMIS) |
| Active | Health workers search for cases (AFP, measles) |
| Sentinel | Selected sites (influenza, HIV) |
| Syndromic | Fever, diarrhoea, respiratory syndromes |
| Event-based | Media reports, rumours |
| Laboratory-based | Isolates, antimicrobial resistance |

**Components**

Data collection → Compilation and analysis → Interpretation → Dissemination → Action (feedback)

**Bangladesh**

IEDCR (national surveillance, outbreak response); AFP, measles, dengue surveillance; DHIS2; International Health Regulations (2005).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Concept of Health and Disease).

## DU.80
**Definitions**

1. The goals of medicine: to **promote** health, **preserve** health, **restore** health when impaired, and **minimise suffering**; these goals are summed up in the word **prevention**.
2. **Prevention**: actions aimed at eradicating, eliminating or minimising the impact of disease and disability, or, if none is feasible, slowing their progress (Last, Dictionary of Epidemiology).

**Concept**

- Based on knowledge of the **natural history of disease**.
- Acts at every stage, not only before disease.

**Levels**

Primordial → Primary → Secondary → Tertiary

**Modes of intervention**

Health promotion; specific protection; early diagnosis and treatment; disability limitation; rehabilitation.

**Examples**

| Level | Example |
|---|---|
| Primordial | Preventing children from starting smoking |
| Primary | Measles vaccination |
| Secondary | Screening for cervical cancer (VIA) |
| Tertiary | Physiotherapy after stroke |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Concept of Health and Disease).

## DU.81
**Definition**

**Community diagnosis**: identification and quantification of the health problems of a community in terms of mortality and morbidity rates and ratios, and identification of their correlates (determinants), in order to define those at risk or in need of health care.

It is to the community what clinical diagnosis is to the patient.

**Steps**

Collect data (census, records, surveys) → Calculate rates and ratios → Identify main problems and at-risk groups → Find causes / correlates → Set priorities → Plan action (community treatment)

**Data used**

Demographic (age-sex structure), vital statistics (births, deaths), morbidity, nutritional status, environment (water, sanitation), socio-economic status, health services and their use.

**Example**

A village survey shows high diarrhoea and stunting among under-fives, linked with unsafe water, open defecation and poor feeding → diagnosis: "diarrhoea and undernutrition due to poor WASH and feeding practices".

**Importance in prevention of disease**

1. **Identifies priority problems and their causes** → targets prevention where it is needed most.
2. **Defines high-risk groups** (mothers, under-fives, elderly, workers) → selective protection and screening.
3. **Basis for planning and resource allocation** of preventive programmes (immunisation, WASH, nutrition).
4. **Baseline for evaluation**: later surveys show whether prevention worked.
5. Detects the **hidden iceberg** of disease missed by hospital data.
6. Promotes **community participation** in solving local problems.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Man and Medicine; Principles of Epidemiology).

## DU.82
**Definition**

**Community treatment**: organised, collective health action taken by the health system and the community to deal with the health problems identified by **community diagnosis**; it includes promotive, preventive, curative and rehabilitative measures applied to the whole population or groups.

Community diagnosis → Community treatment → Evaluation

**Methods**

1. Health education and behaviour change.
2. Environmental measures: safe water, sanitation, vector control.
3. Mass immunisation campaigns.
4. **Mass drug administration** and chemoprophylaxis.
5. Nutrition programmes: supplementation, fortification.
6. Screening and case finding.
7. Legislation: food safety, tobacco control.
8. Organisation of health services and referral.

**Role in controlling disease**

| Disease | Community treatment |
|---|---|
| Lymphatic filariasis | Annual MDA with DEC + albendazole → eliminated in Bangladesh (2023) |
| Polio | National Immunisation Days |
| Cholera | Chlorination, ORS, oral cholera vaccine campaigns |
| Worm infestation | Mass deworming of school children |
| Vitamin A deficiency | Biannual vitamin A campaigns |
| Dengue | Community source reduction |
| Iodine deficiency | Universal salt iodisation |

**Clinical vs community treatment**

| Clinical | Community |
|---|---|
| Individual patient | Whole population |
| Drugs, surgery | Programmes, policy, environment |
| Cure | Prevention and control |
| Evaluated by recovery | Evaluated by fall in incidence |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Man and Medicine).

## DU.83
**Definition**

**Screening**: the search for unrecognised disease or defect by means of rapidly applied tests, examinations or other procedures in apparently healthy individuals. It sorts out those who **probably have** the disease from those who probably do not; it is **not diagnostic** (positives need diagnostic confirmation) (US Commission on Chronic Illness).

**Classification (types of screening)**

| Type | Meaning | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Mass screening** | Whole population or large group, regardless of risk | Hypertension screening of all adults in a community |
| **High-risk / selective screening** | Only groups at higher risk | Diabetes in obese adults; cervical cancer screening in women 30–60 years |
| **Multiphasic screening** | Several tests at one time | Health check-up: BP, glucose, lipids, Hb, urine |
| **Opportunistic (case finding)** | Testing patients who come for other reasons | Checking BP of every OPD patient |

**By purpose (uses)**

1. **Case detection** (prescriptive screening): for the person's benefit (neonatal hypothyroidism, breast cancer).
2. **Control of disease** (prospective screening): to protect others (TB, STIs in sex workers, HBsAg in blood donors).
3. **Research**: natural history of chronic disease.
4. **Educational opportunity**: raising awareness.

**Criteria of a good screening test**

Acceptability; repeatability (reliability); validity (sensitivity, specificity); yield; simplicity; safety; rapidity; ease of administration; low cost.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Screening for Disease).

## DU.84
**2 × 2 table**

| Screening test | Disease present | Disease absent | Total |
|---|---|---|---|
| Positive | a (true positive) | b (false positive) | a + b |
| Negative | c (false negative) | d (true negative) | c + d |
| Total | a + c | b + d | N |

**Sensitivity vs specificity**

| Point | Sensitivity | Specificity |
|---|---|---|
| Definition | Ability of a test to identify correctly those **who have** the disease | Ability of a test to identify correctly those **who do not have** the disease |
| Formula | a / (a + c) × 100 | d / (b + d) × 100 |
| Also called | True positive rate | True negative rate |
| Linked error | Low sensitivity → more **false negatives** | Low specificity → more **false positives** |
| Effect of error | Cases missed; false reassurance; disease spreads | Healthy people investigated, anxious, extra cost |
| Preferred when | Disease serious, treatable, missing it is costly (e.g., blood donor HIV, PKU) | False positives are harmful or costly (e.g., before surgery or toxic treatment); disease rare |
| Effect of moving cut-off (e.g., lowering glucose cut-off) | Increases | Decreases |

**Relationship**

- Sensitivity and specificity are **inversely related** when the cut-off changes; both are inherent properties of the test, not affected by prevalence.
- Two tests in **series** raise specificity; in **parallel** raise sensitivity.

**Related measures**

Positive predictive value = a / (a + b); negative predictive value = d / (c + d); these depend on prevalence.

**Example**

100 diseased, 900 healthy; test positive in 90 diseased and 45 healthy → sensitivity = 90/100 = 90%; specificity = 855/900 = 95%.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Screening for Disease).

## DU.85
**Definition**

**Medical sociology**: the branch of sociology that studies the **social aspects of health, illness and medical care**: social causes and consequences of disease, health and illness behaviour, doctor–patient relationship, medical profession, hospitals and health care systems.

**Two branches (R. Straus, 1957)**

| Branch | Focus | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Sociology in medicine** | Uses sociological knowledge to solve medical and health problems | Social factors in TB, illness behaviour, compliance |
| **Sociology of medicine** | Studies medicine itself as a social institution | Medical profession, hospital organisation, power and ethics |

**Scope**

1. Social factors in **aetiology** of disease (poverty, stress, customs).
2. **Health, illness and sick-role behaviour**.
3. **Doctor–patient relationship** and communication.
4. **Hospital** as a social organisation.
5. **Health care systems**, policy, equity.
6. **Culture, beliefs** and health practices; stigma.
7. Social problems: alcoholism, drug abuse, violence.

**Importance for doctors**

Understands patients in their social setting; improves communication, compliance and prevention; basis of **social medicine** and the holistic approach.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medicine and Social Sciences).

## DU.86
**Components of medical sociology**

1. **Social aetiology of disease**: poverty, overcrowding, illiteracy, occupation, urbanisation, stress.
2. **Social epidemiology**: distribution of disease by class, gender, ethnicity, residence.
3. **Health behaviour, illness behaviour and sick role** (Parsons): rights and duties of the sick.
4. **Doctor–patient relationship**: communication, trust, compliance.
5. **Hospital sociology**: hospital as a social system; roles of staff and patients.
6. **Medical profession**: professional values, ethics, power, socialisation of doctors.
7. **Health care organisation and policy**: access, equity, financing.
8. **Culture and health**: beliefs, customs, taboos, traditional healers.
9. **Family and health**: family's role in causation, care, rehabilitation.
10. **Stigma and deviance**: leprosy, HIV, mental illness.
11. **Stress and social support**.
12. **Social problems**: alcoholism, drug addiction, suicide, delinquency, domestic violence.
13. **Social change**: industrialisation, migration, ageing.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medicine and Social Sciences).

## DU.87
**Factors influencing doctor–patient relationship**

| Group | Factors |
|---|---|
| **Doctor** | Knowledge and competence; communication skills; empathy, courtesy; attitude (respect vs arrogance); time given; honesty; fees; gender; language; professional ethics |
| **Patient** | Education, culture, beliefs; expectations; socio-economic status; anxiety, fear; previous experience; compliance; gender, age |
| **Illness** | Severity, urgency, chronicity; stigma (HIV, mental illness, leprosy); prognosis |
| **Communication** | Language barrier; medical jargon; non-verbal cues; listening; explanation |
| **Setting / system** | Overcrowded OPD, short consultation time, lack of privacy, long waiting; cost of care; public vs private; referral problems |
| **Social** | Media reports, mistrust, commercialisation, violence against doctors, litigation |
| **Third parties** | Family members, attendants, brokers, drug companies |

**Models (Szasz and Hollender)**

| Model | Doctor | Patient | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| Activity–passivity | Active | Passive | Unconscious patient, emergency |
| Guidance–cooperation | Guides | Cooperates | Acute infection |
| Mutual participation | Helps patient help himself | Partner | Chronic disease (diabetes) |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medicine and Social Sciences).

## DU.88
**Strategies to enhance doctor–patient relationship (Bangladesh)**

**A. Doctor-level**

1. **Communication skills** training (undergraduate and in-service): listening, explaining, breaking bad news.
2. Speak **Bangla / local language**; avoid jargon; use **interpreters** for indigenous, Rohingya or foreign patients.
3. **Empathy**, courtesy, respect for dignity, culture and religion.
4. **Informed consent** and shared decision-making.
5. **Privacy and confidentiality**: screened examination, female chaperone.
6. Honest, transparent information on diagnosis, cost, prognosis.
7. Rational prescribing; avoid unnecessary tests; declare conflicts of interest.
8. Follow BMDC code of ethics.

**B. System-level**

1. Reduce **OPD overcrowding**: more doctors, appointment and token systems, strong referral.
2. Adequate **consultation time**.
3. Clear, fixed **fees** in private sector; regulation of private clinics.
4. Patient **help desks**, information boards, complaint and feedback systems.
5. **Security** of doctors and staff; legal protection against violence.
6. Continuity of care: **family physicians**, community clinics.
7. Female doctors and women-friendly services.
8. Telemedicine and follow-up by phone.

**C. Community-level**

1. Health education to set realistic expectations.
2. Responsible media reporting.
3. Community participation in hospital management committees.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medicine and Social Sciences).

## DU.89
**Definition**

**Behaviour**: everything a person does (physical, mental or verbal) in response to internal or external stimuli; observable actions plus internal processes (thinking, feeling).

**Classification**

| Basis | Types | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| Observability | **Overt** (observable) / **Covert** (internal) | Walking, speaking / thinking, feeling |
| Control | **Voluntary** / **Involuntary** (reflex) | Eating / knee jerk, blinking |
| Awareness | **Conscious** / **Unconscious** | Planned decision / slips of tongue, habits |
| Origin | **Innate** (instinctive) / **Learned** (acquired) | Sucking reflex / hand-washing, smoking |
| Normality | **Normal** / **Abnormal** | Social interaction / hallucinatory behaviour |
| Numbers | **Individual** / **Group (collective)** | Personal diet / crowd panic, mob behaviour |
| Effect | **Adaptive** / **Maladaptive** | Seeking help when ill / drug abuse |
| Health-related (Kasl and Cobb) | **Health behaviour**, **illness behaviour**, **sick-role behaviour** | Vaccination; consulting a doctor; taking prescribed medicine |

**Determinants**

Heredity, learning, motivation, perception, attitudes, beliefs, personality, culture, family, peers, environment.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medicine and Social Sciences).

## DU.90
**Types of health-related behaviour (Kasl and Cobb, 1966)**

| Point | Health behaviour | Illness behaviour | Sick-role behaviour |
|---|---|---|---|
| Person's state | Believes himself **healthy** | **Feels ill**; not yet diagnosed | **Considers himself ill** (diagnosed) |
| Purpose | Prevent disease or detect it in asymptomatic stage | Define the illness and find a remedy | Get well |
| Activities | Immunisation, exercise, balanced diet, hand-washing, check-ups, screening | Noticing symptoms, consulting family, self-medication, visiting a doctor | Taking prescribed drugs, rest, follow-up, exemption from duties |
| Example | Woman getting VIA screening | Man with cough visiting the community clinic | TB patient completing DOTS |
| Level of prevention | Primary | Secondary | Secondary / tertiary |

**Other classification**

| Type | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Health-promoting / protective** | Breastfeeding, seat-belt use, safe sex |
| **Health-damaging (risk) behaviour** | Smoking, alcohol, drug use, unsafe sex, open defecation |
| **Help-seeking behaviour** | Choice of provider: doctor, quack, kabiraj |
| **Compliance (adherence)** | Following treatment advice |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medicine and Social Sciences).

## DU.91
**Human needs**

Needs are states of deprivation that motivate behaviour.

| Type | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Primary (biological)** | Food, water, air, sleep, shelter, sex, avoidance of pain |
| **Secondary (psychosocial, learned)** | Love, security, belonging, recognition, achievement, power |

**Maslow's hierarchy of needs (1943)**

Needs are arranged in a pyramid; lower needs must be met before higher ones motivate.

| Level (bottom → top) | Need | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Physiological** | Food, water, sleep, shelter |
| 2 | **Safety / security** | Physical safety, job security, health, money |
| 3 | **Love and belonging (social)** | Family, friends, acceptance in a group |
| 4 | **Esteem** | Self-respect, recognition, status, achievement |
| 5 | **Self-actualisation** | Realising one's full potential, creativity |

Physiological → Safety → Belonging → Esteem → Self-actualisation

- Levels 1–4: **deficiency needs**; level 5: **growth need**.

**Relevance to health**

1. Poor people focused on survival needs may ignore preventive services → link health with basic needs.
2. Motivating health workers: pay meets lower needs; recognition and growth meet higher needs.
3. Patient care: safety, belonging and dignity matter, not only treatment.
4. Health education must appeal to the need active in the audience.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medicine and Social Sciences; Communication for Health Education).

## DU.92
**How behaviour influences health**

Health depends largely on what people do; many leading causes of death are **behaviour-related**.

| Behaviour | Effect on health |
|---|---|
| Tobacco smoking and chewing | Lung and oral cancers, CHD, stroke, COPD, TB |
| Unhealthy diet (salt, sugar, fat) | Hypertension, diabetes, obesity, CHD |
| Physical inactivity | Obesity, diabetes, CHD, osteoporosis, depression |
| Alcohol and drug use | Liver disease, accidents, violence, HIV (injecting) |
| Unsafe sex | STIs, HIV, unwanted pregnancy |
| Poor hygiene, open defecation | Diarrhoea, typhoid, hepatitis, worms |
| Delayed care-seeking | Complications, maternal and child deaths |
| Non-adherence to treatment | Treatment failure, drug resistance (MDR-TB) |
| Reckless driving, no helmet | Road traffic injuries |
| Breastfeeding, immunisation, ANC | Protection of mother and child |
| Regular exercise, balanced diet, sleep | Physical and mental well-being |

**Pathways**

1. **Direct**: exposure to or protection from risk (smoking → cancer).
2. **Through use of services**: screening, vaccination, early treatment.
3. **Through others**: second-hand smoke; spread of infection; children copying parents.
4. **Psychological**: stress-coping behaviour affects mental and physical health.

**Implications**

Behaviour change communication; health belief model; laws and policy; community participation; positive role models.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medicine and Social Sciences; Communication for Health Education).

## DU.93
**a) Analysis using Maslow's hierarchy (1.5)**

- Adequate salary satisfies **physiological** and **safety** needs (food, shelter, security).
- He is still dissatisfied → the unmet needs are higher:
  1. **Esteem needs** (most likely): lack of recognition, respect, appreciation, status, sense of achievement; interns often feel undervalued.
  2. **Love and belonging (social)**: may not feel accepted by the team; isolation, poor relations with seniors.
  3. **Self-actualisation**: little chance to learn, grow and use skills; routine clerical work.
- Herzberg's two-factor view agrees: salary is a **hygiene factor** (prevents dissatisfaction) but does not motivate; **motivators** are recognition, responsibility, achievement and growth.

**b) Effect on professional performance (2)**

1. **Low motivation** and job satisfaction; minimal effort.
2. **Poor quality of care**: hurried history, errors, poor record keeping.
3. **Poor communication** and empathy with patients → weak doctor–patient relationship.
4. **Absenteeism**, late arrival, early leaving.
5. **Burnout**, stress, frustration, depression.
6. Poor **teamwork** and conflicts with staff.
7. Little interest in learning → slow skill development.
8. Wish to leave the job or profession (turnover, brain drain).

**Remedies**

Recognition and appreciation; constructive feedback; supportive supervision and mentoring; involvement in decisions; clear roles; learning opportunities; good team culture.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medicine and Social Sciences; Health Planning and Management).

## DU.94
**Definition**

**Learning**: a relatively permanent change in behaviour (knowledge, attitude or skill) that results from **experience or practice**.

Changes due to maturation, fatigue, illness or drugs are **not** learning.

**Features**

1. A **change in behaviour** (actual or potential).
2. **Relatively permanent**.
3. Result of **experience, practice or training**.
4. Can be good or bad (hand-washing or smoking are both learned).
5. Continuous, lifelong process.
6. Goal-directed; influenced by motivation.

**Process**

Stimulus → Perception → Response → Reinforcement (reward) → Learned behaviour

**Domains (Bloom)**

Cognitive (knowledge), affective (attitude), psychomotor (skill).

**Importance in health**

Basis of health education and behaviour change; training of health workers; patient self-care (insulin injection, ORS preparation).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medicine and Social Sciences; Communication for Health Education).

## DU.95
**Types of learning**

| Type | Proponent | Mechanism | Example |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Classical conditioning** | Pavlov | Neutral stimulus paired with natural stimulus produces a conditioned response | Child cries at the sight of a white coat after injections |
| **Operant (instrumental) conditioning** | Skinner | Behaviour shaped by its consequences (reward or punishment) | Praise for completed vaccination card encourages return visits |
| **Trial and error** | Thorndike | Repeated attempts; successful responses retained | Learning to use an inhaler correctly |
| **Insight (cognitive) learning** | Köhler (Gestalt) | Sudden understanding of relationships | Grasping why ORS works |
| **Observational / social learning** | Bandura | Imitation of a model | Adolescents copying smoking parents; peer educators |
| **Rote learning** | — | Memorising by repetition | Drug doses |
| **Meaningful learning** | Ausubel | Linking new to existing knowledge | Understanding pathophysiology |

**Other classifications**

- By domain (Bloom): cognitive, affective, psychomotor.
- By setting: formal (school), non-formal (training), informal (daily life).
- By participation: active (learning by doing) vs passive (listening).
- By group: individual vs group learning.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medicine and Social Sciences; Communication for Health Education).

## DU.96
**Factors affecting learning**

| Group | Factors |
|---|---|
| **Learner** | Motivation and interest; intelligence; age; physical and mental health; attention; readiness; previous knowledge; emotional state (anxiety); fatigue; sensory defects |
| **Teacher** | Knowledge; teaching method; communication; attitude; use of examples and aids |
| **Material** | Meaningfulness; difficulty; length; organisation (simple → complex) |
| **Method** | Practice and repetition; spaced vs massed practice; active participation (learning by doing); feedback; reinforcement (reward); audio-visual aids |
| **Environment** | Physical (light, ventilation, noise, seating); social (group size, peer influence); culture |

**Defence mechanisms**

Unconscious mental processes that protect the self from anxiety (Freud).

| Mechanism | Example |
|---|---|
| Repression | Forgetting a painful event |
| Denial | Refusing to accept a cancer diagnosis |
| Projection | Blaming the doctor for one's own failure |
| Rationalisation | "Smoking relieves my stress" |
| Displacement | Anger at boss shown to family |
| Regression | Adult behaving childishly in hospital |
| Reaction formation | Excess kindness to a disliked person |
| Sublimation | Aggression channelled into sport |
| Identification | Student copying a respected teacher |
| Intellectualisation | Discussing one's illness only in technical terms |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Medicine and Social Sciences; Communication for Health Education).

## DU.97
**Bloom's domains of learning (Bloom, 1956)**

| Domain | Concerns | Levels (lowest → highest) | Medical example |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Cognitive** | Knowledge, thinking ("head") | Remember → Understand → Apply → Analyse → Evaluate → Create (revised 2001; original: knowledge, comprehension, application, analysis, synthesis, evaluation) | Knowing EPI schedule; interpreting a growth chart |
| **Affective** | Attitudes, feelings, values ("heart") | Receiving → Responding → Valuing → Organisation → Characterisation (Krathwohl) | Showing empathy; valuing confidentiality |
| **Psychomotor** | Manual and physical skills ("hands") | Imitation → Manipulation → Precision → Articulation → Naturalisation (Dave) | Giving an injection; taking BP; CPR |

**Uses**

1. Writing clear **learning objectives** (knowledge, attitude, skill).
2. Choosing **teaching methods**: lectures (cognitive), role play (affective), demonstration and practice (psychomotor).
3. Designing **assessment**: MCQ/SAQ, attitude scales, OSCE.
4. Health education aims at all three: KAP (knowledge, attitude, practice).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Communication for Health Education).

## DU.98
**Key principles of learning**

1. **Motivation**: learning is fastest when the learner wants to learn (felt need).
2. **Readiness** (Thorndike's law of readiness): learner must be physically and mentally ready.
3. **Exercise / practice**: repetition strengthens learning ("practice makes perfect").
4. **Effect / reinforcement**: rewarded responses are repeated; punished ones fade.
5. **Active participation**: "learning by doing" is better than passive listening.
6. **Known to unknown; simple to complex; concrete to abstract**.
