| Gas | Percentage |
|---|---|
| Nitrogen | ~78% |
| Oxygen | ~21% |
| Argon | ~0.93% |
| Carbon dioxide | ~0.04% |
| Others (neon, helium, hydrogen, ozone) | Traces |
| Water vapour | Variable |

**B. Physical (thermal comfort) components**

1. Air temperature
2. Humidity
3. Air movement (velocity)
4. Radiation (mean radiant temperature)
Indices: corrected effective temperature, predicted 4-hour sweat rate.

**C. Purity (pollutant) components**

1. Particulate matter (PM₂.₅, PM₁₀)
2. Gases: CO, SO₂, NO₂, ozone
3. Lead and other metals
4. Volatile organic compounds
5. Biological agents: bacteria, viruses, pollen, moulds
6. Odour

**D. Ventilation**

Adequate fresh air supply and removal of contaminated air (air changes per hour).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.227
**Major air pollutants**

| Pollutant | Main sources | Health effects |
|---|---|---|
| **Particulate matter (PM₂.₅, PM₁₀)**, smoke, soot | Vehicles, brick kilns, construction, burning | Respiratory and heart disease, lung cancer |
| **Carbon monoxide (CO)** | Incomplete combustion; vehicles, stoves | Carboxyhaemoglobin, hypoxia, death |
| **Sulphur dioxide (SO₂)** | Coal and oil burning, industry, kilns | Bronchospasm, acid rain |
| **Nitrogen oxides (NO, NO₂)** | Vehicles, power plants | Airway irritation, smog |
| **Ozone (O₃)** (secondary) | Sunlight + NOx + hydrocarbons | Reduced lung function, asthma |
| **Hydrocarbons / VOCs** (benzene) | Vehicles, solvents | Cancer, smog formation |
| **Lead** | Leaded fuel (past), industry, batteries | Anaemia, neurotoxicity in children |
| Fluorides | Aluminium, fertiliser industries | Fluorosis |
| Other metals (cadmium, arsenic, mercury) | Industry | Organ toxicity, cancer |
| Biological (pollen, spores, microbes) | Plants, waste | Allergy, infections |

**Criteria pollutants (WHO / national standards)**

PM₂.₅, PM₁₀, O₃, NO₂, SO₂, CO (and lead).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.228
**Three important sources of indoor air pollution in Bangladesh**

1. **Burning of solid biomass fuels** (wood, crop residues, cow-dung cakes, coal) for cooking in poorly ventilated kitchens: smoke, PM₂.₅, CO.
2. **Tobacco smoking** indoors (second-hand smoke).
3. **Mosquito coils** and kerosene lamps or stoves.

**Other sources**

Incense; cooking oil fumes; dampness and moulds; building materials and paints; cleaning chemicals; asbestos; poor ventilation in crowded slums.

**Most affected**

Women who cook, and young children kept near the stove → pneumonia, COPD, low birth weight.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.229
**Ventilation**

Replacement of vitiated (used) air by fresh outdoor air, to maintain comfort and remove heat, moisture, odours and contaminants.

**Categories**

| Type | Mechanism / methods |
|---|---|
| **A. Natural ventilation** | |
| 1. Wind (perflation) | Wind blows through openings (doors, windows); **cross-ventilation** through opposite openings |
| 2. Diffusion | Slow passage of air through openings and porous walls |
| 3. Inequality of temperature (stack effect) | Warm air rises and leaves through high openings; cool air enters low |
| **B. Artificial (mechanical) ventilation** | |
| 1. Exhaust (extraction) ventilation | Fans remove vitiated air; fresh air enters by openings (kitchens, toilets, factories) |
| 2. Plenum (supply / propulsion) ventilation | Fresh air blown in; foul air escapes by vents |
| 3. Balanced ventilation | Combination of supply and exhaust |
| 4. Air conditioning | Controls temperature, humidity, air movement and purity |

**Standards (Park)**

Air change: 2–3 per hour in living rooms (more in workplaces); window area at least 1/10 of floor area (doors + windows ~2/5); adequate cubic space per person.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.230
**Types of artificial (mechanical) ventilation**

| Type | Description | Uses |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Exhaust (extraction) ventilation** | Electric fans or blowers draw out vitiated air; fresh air enters through doors, windows and inlets | Kitchens, toilets, factories, laboratories (local exhaust hoods over dust or fumes) |
| **2. Plenum (supply / propulsion) ventilation** | Fresh air (often filtered, cooled or heated) blown in by fans; used air escapes through outlets | Theatres, large halls, mines, operation theatres (positive pressure) |
| **3. Balanced ventilation** | Combination of supply and exhaust systems, matched to keep correct pressure | Large buildings, hospitals, isolation rooms (negative pressure for TB/airborne infections) |
| **4. Air conditioning** | Controls temperature, humidity, air movement and air purity (filtration) simultaneously | Hospitals, offices, ICUs, cinemas |

**Other devices**

Ceiling and table fans (air movement only; no fresh air exchange); air coolers; HEPA filters and air purifiers.

**Advantages**

Independent of wind and temperature; controllable; filtration possible.

**Disadvantages**

Cost; power needed; noise; poor maintenance can spread infection (Legionella).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.231
**Simplest system: natural cross-ventilation**

Openings (windows / doors) on **opposite walls** + **ventilators** high on the walls near the ceiling.

**Diagram (section of a room)**

```
   Outlet ventilator (high, near ceiling) → warm, used air out
  ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │ ▒▒                       ▒▒  │
  │                              │
  │  ← fresh air      warm air ↑ │
  │                              │
Window ▶   →  →  →  →  →  →    ◀ Window (opposite wall)
  │  (inlet, lower level)        │
  └──────────────────────────────┘
```

Fresh air enters through the lower window on the windward side → crosses the room → warm, used air rises and leaves through high ventilators and the opposite window.

**Principles**

1. **Perflation**: wind through opposite openings.
2. **Stack effect**: warm air rises and escapes at the top; cool air enters below.
3. Inlets low, outlets high.
4. Openings unobstructed; window area at least 1/10 of floor area.

**Advantages**

Cheap, no power needed, simple; suits rural and low-income homes in Bangladesh.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.232
**Noise**

**Wrong sound, in the wrong place, at the wrong time**; any unwanted sound that annoys or harms.

**Properties**

| Property | Unit | Note |
|---|---|---|
| Intensity (loudness) | Decibel (dB) | Logarithmic; +10 dB ≈ 10 times intensity |
| Frequency (pitch) | Hertz (Hz) | Human hearing 20–20,000 Hz; most sensitive 2,000–5,000 Hz |
| Duration | Time | Longer exposure → more damage |

Hearing damage risk: prolonged exposure **above 85 dB(A)** (8 hours/day).

**Sources of noise**

| Source | Examples |
|---|---|
| Traffic | Horns (hydraulic horns), engines, buses, motorcycles, trains |
| Industry | Machines, generators, looms, presses, metal workshops |
| Construction | Pile drivers, drills, mixers |
| Aircraft | Airports, flight paths |
| Social / community | Loudspeakers and microphones (*miking*), festivals, firecrackers, weddings, political rallies |
| Domestic | Television, music, appliances, generators |
| Commercial | Markets, shops with speakers |

**Typical levels**

Whisper ~30 dB; conversation ~60 dB; heavy traffic ~85–90 dB; hydraulic horn 100+ dB; jet engine ~140 dB.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.233
**Effects of noise pollution on health**

**A. Auditory effects**

1. **Auditory fatigue** (temporary threshold shift): above ~90 dB, recovers with rest.
2. **Noise-induced hearing loss** (permanent threshold shift): prolonged exposure >85 dB; first at **4,000 Hz** ("4 kHz notch"); irreversible.
3. **Acoustic trauma**: sudden very loud noise (>140 dB, blasts) → ruptured eardrum.
4. **Tinnitus**.

**B. Non-auditory effects**

| Effect | Details |
|---|---|
| Interference with speech | Communication difficulty; accidents from missed warnings |
| Annoyance | Irritation, anger |
| Sleep disturbance | Insomnia, fatigue |
| Reduced efficiency | Poor concentration and work output; more errors |
| Physiological | Rise in BP and heart rate, hypertension, CVD, headache, raised intracranial pressure, increased sweating, nausea, vertigo |
| Psychological | Stress, anxiety, neurosis, aggression |
| Children | Impaired learning and reading; delayed speech |
| Pregnancy | Possible low birth weight with high exposure |

**Vulnerable groups**

Traffic police, drivers, factory and construction workers, children, elderly, patients in hospitals.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health; Occupational Health).

## DU.234
**Noise control measures in Dhaka city**

**A. Legislation and enforcement**

1. **Noise Pollution (Control) Rules 2006**: zone-wise day and night limits (silent, residential, mixed, commercial, industrial zones).
2. **Silent zones** around hospitals, schools, offices and courts; no horns.
3. **Ban on hydraulic horns**; mobile courts and fines.
4. Permits and time limits for loudspeakers and construction at night.

**B. At the source**

1. Vehicle fitness checks; silencers; ban on old noisy vehicles.
2. Traffic management to reduce congestion and honking.
3. Low-noise machinery; enclosures for generators and industrial machines.
4. Construction work only in daytime; quieter equipment.

**C. Along the path**

1. **Zoning**: industries and workshops away from homes, schools and hospitals.
2. **Green belts** and trees; noise barriers along highways and flyovers.
3. Sound insulation of buildings; double-glazed windows in hospitals.

**D. At the receiver**

Ear plugs and muffs for workers and traffic police; periodic audiometry.

**E. Education and awareness**

Campaigns against unnecessary honking; driver training; community participation; school education.

**F. Monitoring**

Regular noise measurement by Department of Environment; mapping noise hotspots.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.235
**a) Common sources of noise in Chawkbazar (2)**

1. **Traffic**: honking (including hydraulic horns), rickshaw and vehicle congestion, trucks loading goods.
2. **Small factories and workshops**: plastic, metal, printing and packaging units; machines and generators.
3. **Commercial activities**: wholesale markets, shop loudspeakers, hawkers.
4. **Construction** and demolition.
5. **Social events**: loudspeakers, processions, religious and festive gatherings.

**b) Health effects of prolonged excessive noise (2)**

1. **Noise-induced hearing loss** (permanent; first at 4 kHz); tinnitus.
2. **Hypertension**, cardiovascular disease, headache.
3. **Sleep disturbance**, fatigue.
4. **Stress, irritability, anxiety**, reduced concentration and work efficiency.
5. **Speech interference** → accidents.
6. Impaired learning in children.

**c) Control measures by the local authority (3)**

1. **Enforce Noise Pollution (Control) Rules 2006**: zone limits; fines by mobile courts.
2. **Ban hydraulic horns**; declare no-horn zones near schools, hospitals and mosques.
3. **Traffic management**: one-way routes, fixed loading/unloading hours, rickshaw lanes.
4. **Relocate or zone** noisy factories and workshops away from residential areas; require sound-proof enclosures for generators and machines.
5. **Regulate loudspeakers** and construction hours (no night work).
6. **Noise barriers and tree planting**.
7. **Regular noise monitoring** and public display of levels.
8. **Awareness campaigns** for drivers, traders and residents; ear protection for exposed workers.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.236
**Sources of radiation exposure**

**A. Natural (background) sources**: the largest share of human exposure.

| Source | Examples |
|---|---|
| Cosmic rays | From outer space; higher at high altitude and in air travel |
| Terrestrial | Uranium, thorium, radium in soil and rocks; building materials |
| **Radon** gas | From soil into houses; main natural source |
| Internal | Potassium-40, carbon-14 in the body; from food and water |

**B. Man-made (artificial) sources**

| Source | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Medical** (largest man-made source) | Diagnostic X-ray, CT scan, fluoroscopy, nuclear medicine (isotope scans), radiotherapy |
| Occupational | Radiology staff, nuclear workers, industrial radiography, miners |
| Nuclear power and fuel cycle | Reactors (e.g., Rooppur nuclear power plant), waste |
| Nuclear accidents and weapon tests | Chernobyl, Fukushima; fallout |
| Consumer products | Luminous dials, smoke detectors, tobacco (polonium-210) |
| Industrial and research uses | Radioisotopes, sterilisation of medical products |

**C. Non-ionising radiation**

Ultraviolet (sun), visible light, infrared, microwaves, radiofrequency (mobile phones), lasers.

**Units**

Absorbed dose: gray (Gy); effective dose: sievert (Sv).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health; Occupational Health).

## DU.237
**Radiation hazards**

**A. Ionising radiation**

| Type | Effects |
|---|---|
| **Somatic: immediate (deterministic)** | Acute radiation syndrome; skin erythema, burns, epilation; sterility; cataract; bone marrow depression; fetal death |
| **Somatic: delayed (stochastic)** | **Cancers**: leukaemia, thyroid, breast, lung, bone, skin cancer; shortened life span |
| **Genetic** | Mutations in germ cells → congenital defects in descendants |
| **In utero exposure** | Abortion, microcephaly, intellectual disability, growth retardation, childhood cancer |

**Deterministic vs stochastic**

| Deterministic | Stochastic |
|---|---|
| Threshold dose exists | No threshold |
| Severity increases with dose | Probability increases with dose |
| e.g., burns, cataract, ARS | e.g., cancer, genetic effects |

**Factors affecting harm**

Dose, dose rate, type of radiation, part of body exposed, age (children and fetus most sensitive), cell sensitivity (rapidly dividing cells: bone marrow, gut, gonads, skin).

**B. Non-ionising radiation**

UV: sunburn, skin cancer, cataract, snow blindness; infrared: heat cataract (glass-blowers), burns; microwaves: tissue heating; lasers: retinal burns.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health; Occupational Health).

## DU.238
**Acute radiation syndrome (ARS)**

An acute illness caused by **whole-body (or most of body) exposure to a high dose of penetrating ionising radiation (>1 Gy) over a short time** (minutes).

**Phases**

Prodromal (nausea, vomiting, fatigue: minutes–days) → Latent (apparent recovery) → Manifest illness → Recovery or death

**Syndromes by dose**

| Syndrome | Dose (approx.) | Features |
|---|---|---|
| **Haematopoietic (bone marrow)** | 1–8 Gy | Pancytopenia → infections, bleeding, anaemia; death in weeks without treatment |
| **Gastrointestinal** | ~6–10 Gy or more | Severe vomiting, bloody diarrhoea, dehydration, sepsis; usually fatal |
| **Cardiovascular / CNS** | >20–50 Gy | Confusion, convulsions, coma, shock; death within hours–days |

- **LD₅₀/₆₀** (dose killing 50% in 60 days) without treatment: about 3.5–4 Gy.
- **Cutaneous radiation injury** often accompanies ARS.

**Management**

Decontamination; supportive care (fluids, antiemetics); antibiotics; blood products; growth factors (G-CSF); bone marrow transplant in selected cases; potassium iodide for radioiodine exposure.

**Prevention**

Radiation safety, emergency preparedness at nuclear facilities.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.239
**Principles of radiation protection (ICRP)**

1. **Justification**: every exposure must do more good than harm.
2. **Optimisation (ALARA)**: dose kept "as low as reasonably achievable".
3. **Dose limits**: occupational 20 mSv/year (averaged over 5 years, not >50 mSv in any one year); public 1 mSv/year.

**Protection of X-ray technicians and imaging staff**

| Measure | Details |
|---|---|
| **Time** | Minimise time near the source |
| **Distance** | Stay as far as possible (inverse square law: doubling distance → ¼ dose) |
| **Shielding** | Lead aprons, thyroid collars, lead gloves, lead-glass screens and goggles; lead/concrete walls and doors of X-ray rooms |
| **Personal monitoring** | TLD / film badges worn at work; dose records |
| **Equipment safety** | Collimation, filtration, regular calibration and maintenance; no leakage |
| **Safe work practice** | Never hold patients during exposure; use immobilisers; control panel behind shield |
| **Warning signs** | Radiation symbol, red light outside room |
| **Medical surveillance** | Pre-employment and periodic examination, blood counts, eye check |
| **Training** | Radiation safety education |
| **Pregnant staff** | Declare pregnancy; dose to fetus ≤1 mSv for rest of pregnancy |
| **Regulation** | Licensing and inspection (Bangladesh Atomic Energy Regulatory Authority) |

**Protection of patients and public**

Avoid unnecessary or repeat X-rays; use lowest dose; gonadal and thyroid shields; ask about pregnancy (ten-day rule); prefer ultrasound or MRI when suitable.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health; Occupational Health).

## DU.240
**Greenhouse gases (GHGs)**

| Gas | Main sources |
|---|---|
| **Carbon dioxide (CO₂)** | Burning fossil fuels (coal, oil, gas), deforestation, cement |
| **Methane (CH₄)** | Rice paddies, livestock, landfills, gas leaks |
| **Nitrous oxide (N₂O)** | Fertilisers, burning of biomass, industry |
| **Chlorofluorocarbons (CFCs)**, HCFCs, **HFCs** | Refrigerators, air conditioners, aerosols, foam |
| **Water vapour** | Natural evaporation (feedback amplifier) |
| **Tropospheric ozone (O₃)** | Secondary pollutant from vehicles and industry |
| Perfluorocarbons (PFCs), sulphur hexafluoride (SF₆) | Industry, electrical equipment |

**Note**

CO₂ contributes most to warming because of its quantity; methane and N₂O are much more potent per molecule; CFCs also deplete the ozone layer.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.241
**Environmental pollution: the challenge of this millennium**

Environment-related risks cause about a quarter of global deaths and disease (WHO, 2016 estimate for 2012).

**Types and problems**

| Type | Main problems |
|---|---|
| Air pollution | Respiratory and heart disease, lung cancer; Dhaka among the most polluted cities |
| Water pollution | Diarrhoea, cholera, typhoid; arsenic; river pollution (Buriganga, Turag) |
| Soil pollution | Pesticides, heavy metals, industrial waste; enters food chain |
| Noise pollution | Hearing loss, stress, hypertension |
| Plastic and solid waste | Drain blockage, flooding, microplastics |
| E-waste and hospital waste | Toxic metals, infections |
| Chemical pollution | Lead, mercury, pesticides, endocrine disruptors |
| Radiation | Cancer, genetic effects |
| **Climate change** (GHG pollution) | Heat, floods, cyclones, sea-level rise, vector-borne disease |

**Why it is a challenge**

1. **Rapid urbanisation and industrialisation**; population growth.
2. **Global and trans-boundary** nature (air, rivers, climate).
3. **Long-term, cumulative** effects (cancers, climate).
4. **Poor and vulnerable** suffer most → inequity.
5. **Weak enforcement** of laws in developing countries.
6. Conflicts with economic growth; high cost of control.
7. Loss of biodiversity and ecosystem services.

**Responses**

SDGs (3.9: reduce deaths from pollution; 6: water and sanitation; 11: sustainable cities; 13: climate action); Paris Agreement; national laws (Environment Conservation Act 1995); cleaner technologies; waste management; public awareness; intersectoral action.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health); WHO, Preventing disease through healthy environments, 2016.

## DU.242
**Global warming**

The **rise in the average temperature of the earth's surface and lower atmosphere** due to increased concentration of greenhouse gases (enhanced greenhouse effect) from human activities since the industrial revolution.

**Causes**

1. Burning of fossil fuels → CO₂.
2. Deforestation (less CO₂ absorption).
3. Agriculture and livestock (methane, N₂O).
4. Industrial gases (CFCs, HFCs).
5. Urbanisation and land-use change.

**Evidence**

Global surface temperature about 1.1 °C above pre-industrial (1850–1900) levels in 2011–2020 (IPCC AR6, 2021); melting glaciers and ice sheets; rising sea level; more frequent extreme weather.

**Consequences**

Heat waves; sea-level rise; floods, droughts, cyclones; salinity intrusion; crop failure and food insecurity; spread of vector-borne diseases; loss of biodiversity; displacement of people.

**Prevention (mitigation)**

Cut GHG emissions: renewable energy, energy efficiency, public transport, reforestation, less deforestation; international action (UNFCCC, Kyoto Protocol, Paris Agreement 2015: keep warming well below 2 °C, aim 1.5 °C).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.243
**Climate change**

Long-term change in temperature, rainfall and weather patterns, mainly due to human-made greenhouse gas emissions. Bangladesh is among the most vulnerable countries: low-lying delta, dense population, poverty.

**Impact on health in Bangladesh**

| Climate effect | Health impact |
|---|---|
| **Heat waves**, rising temperature | Heat stroke, dehydration, kidney disease, deaths in elderly and outdoor workers; reduced work capacity |
| **Floods** | Drowning, injuries, diarrhoea, cholera, typhoid, hepatitis E, leptospirosis, skin infections, snake bites |
| **Cyclones and storm surges** (Sidr 2007, Aila 2009, Amphan 2020) | Deaths, injuries, destruction of health facilities, displacement |
| **Sea-level rise and salinity** (coastal areas) | Unsafe drinking water; hypertension, pre-eclampsia, skin disease; reduced crop yield |
| **Changing rainfall and temperature** | Spread of **dengue**, chikungunya, malaria to new areas and seasons |
| **Drought and crop loss** | Food insecurity, malnutrition, stunting |
| **Displacement and migration** | Urban slums, overcrowding, poor sanitation, TB, mental stress |
| **Air quality** | Worse air pollution and pollen allergies |
| **Mental health** | Anxiety, depression, PTSD after disasters |

**Greenhouse effect in Bangladesh context**

Bangladesh emits little but suffers most → climate justice.

**Responses**

1. **Adaptation**: cyclone shelters and early warning, flood-resilient WASH, climate-resilient health facilities, disease surveillance, heat action plans, saline-tolerant crops.
2. **Mitigation**: renewable energy (solar home systems), afforestation (coastal green belt), energy efficiency.
3. **Policies**: Bangladesh Climate Change Strategy and Action Plan 2009; National Adaptation Plan 2023–2050; health-sector climate strategy.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health; Disaster Management).

## DU.244
**Ozone layer**

Layer of ozone (O₃) in the **stratosphere** (~15–35 km) that absorbs most harmful **ultraviolet-B (UV-B)** radiation from the sun.

**Depletion**

Caused by **chlorofluorocarbons (CFCs)**, halons, carbon tetrachloride, methyl chloroform, methyl bromide → release chlorine and bromine, which destroy ozone; "ozone hole" over Antarctica.

**Impact on community health**

| Area | Effects |
|---|---|
| **Skin** | Sunburn; **skin cancers**: basal cell, squamous cell carcinoma, malignant melanoma; photo-ageing |
| **Eyes** | **Cataract**, photokeratitis (snow blindness), pterygium, ocular cancers |
| **Immune system** | Suppression → more infections, reduced vaccine response, reactivation of herpes |
| **Food and nutrition** | Reduced crop yield; damage to marine phytoplankton and fisheries → food insecurity |
| **Environment** | Damage to ecosystems; more ground-level ozone and smog |

**Prevention**

1. **Montreal Protocol (1987)**: phase-out of ozone-depleting substances (most successful environmental treaty); Kigali Amendment (2016) phases down HFCs.
2. Use of ozone-friendly refrigerants and aerosols.
3. Personal protection: avoid midday sun, protective clothing, hats, sunglasses, sunscreen.
4. Public UV index warnings and education.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.245
**Greenhouse phenomenon (greenhouse effect)**

The warming of the earth's surface because certain gases in the atmosphere trap heat, like the glass of a greenhouse.

**Mechanism**

Sun's short-wave radiation passes through atmosphere → absorbed by earth's surface → earth emits long-wave **infrared (heat)** radiation → **greenhouse gases** (CO₂, methane, N₂O, CFCs, water vapour, ozone) absorb and re-radiate it back → lower atmosphere and surface warm up

**Natural vs enhanced greenhouse effect**

| Natural | Enhanced (man-made) |
|---|---|
| Keeps average earth temperature about +15 °C instead of about −18 °C; essential for life | Extra GHGs from fossil fuels, deforestation, agriculture trap more heat → **global warming** and climate change |

**Consequences of enhanced effect**

Rising temperature, sea-level rise, extreme weather, floods, droughts, health effects (heat illness, vector-borne and water-borne diseases, malnutrition).

**Control**

Reduce GHG emissions; renewable energy; forests; international agreements (UNFCCC, Paris Agreement).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.246
**Definition**

**Housing** (residential environment, WHO): not only the physical structure that gives shelter, but also its **immediate surroundings** and the related **community services and facilities** needed for the physical, mental and social well-being of the family and individual.

**Components**

1. Physical structure: shelter, rooms, floor, roof, walls.
2. Facilities: water supply, sanitation, drainage, kitchen, lighting, ventilation.
3. Surroundings: neighbourhood, open space, safety, freedom from nuisances.
4. Community services: schools, health facilities, transport, markets, recreation.

**Importance**

Housing is a major determinant of health; poor housing and overcrowding spread TB, respiratory infections, diarrhoea, skin diseases and cause accidents and mental stress.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.247
**Criteria of healthful housing (WHO Expert Committee)**

Healthful housing provides a physical structure that promotes physical, mental and social well-being:
1. Meets **fundamental physiological needs**: protection from weather; adequate light, ventilation, temperature; clean air; space for rest and exercise.
2. Meets **psychological needs**: privacy, comfort, normal family and community life.
3. Protects **against contagion**: safe water, sanitary excreta and refuse disposal, no overcrowding, freedom from vermin.
4. Protects **against accidents**: safe structure, fire safety, electrical safety, safe stairs.

**Housing standards (Park)**

| Item | Standard |
|---|---|
| Site | Elevated, well drained, away from nuisances (dust, smoke, noise, odour) |
| Set-back | Open space around the house for light and air |
| Floor | Impermeable, easy to clean, damp-proof |
| Walls | Strong, smooth, damp-proof, poor heat conductors |
| Roof | Weather-proof; height ≥10 feet |
| Rooms | At least two rooms, with adequate floor space per person |
| Windows | Area at least 1/10 of floor area; doors + windows about 2/5 of floor area |
| Lighting | Adequate daylight in all rooms |
| Kitchen | Separate, with smoke outlet |
| Latrine | Sanitary latrine |
| Water supply | Safe and adequate |
| Drainage, waste disposal | Proper |
| Bathing and washing | Facilities provided |

**Overcrowding standards (persons per room)**

1 room: 2 persons; 2 rooms: 3; 3 rooms: 5; 4 rooms: 7; 5+ rooms: 10 (+2 per extra room). Children <1 year not counted; 1–10 years counted as half.

**"Healthful housing for healthy life"**

Good housing prevents infections (TB, diarrhoea, scabies), accidents and mental stress; supports family life, child development and productivity, so it is a basic need for health.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.248
**Effects of poor housing**

**A. Physical health**

| Housing defect | Health effect |
|---|---|
| Overcrowding, poor ventilation | **Tuberculosis**, common cold, influenza, pneumonia, diphtheria, whooping cough, measles, meningococcal meningitis |
| Poor sanitation, unsafe water | Diarrhoea, cholera, typhoid, hepatitis, worm infestations |
| Crowding, lack of water | Skin infections: scabies, impetigo, fungal infections; lice |
| Rats and insects | Plague, leptospirosis, dengue, malaria, filariasis |
| Dampness | Rheumatism, arthritis, respiratory disease, asthma (moulds) |
| Indoor smoke | ARI in children, COPD in women |
| Poor lighting, unsafe structure | **Accidents**: falls, burns, fires, electrocution; eye strain |
| Lead paint, asbestos | Poisoning, cancer |

**B. Psychological effects**

Stress, frustration, lack of privacy, depression, mental illness, sleep disturbance.

**C. Social effects**

Juvenile delinquency, alcoholism, drug abuse, crime, prostitution, family breakdown, poor school performance.

**D. Mortality**

Higher morbidity and mortality, especially **infant mortality** and TB deaths, in slums.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.249
**Methods of refuse disposal (Park)**

| Method | Description | Remarks |
|---|---|---|
| **1. Dumping** | Refuse dumped in low-lying land; open dumps | Cheap but attracts flies, rats, odour, fires, leachate; unsanitary |
| **2. Controlled tipping / sanitary landfill** | Refuse placed in layers, compacted, covered daily with ~20–30 cm earth; final cover ~60 cm | Most satisfactory for cities where land is available |
| **3. Incineration** | Burning in a furnace | Best for hospital (infectious) waste; costly; air pollution if poorly run |
| **4. Composting** | Refuse + night soil decomposed by bacteria into humus (manure); **Bangalore method** (anaerobic, in trenches) or mechanical composting | Gives manure; suits Indian/Bangladeshi refuse rich in organic matter |
| **5. Manure pits** | Rural households throw refuse and cattle dung into pits; covered with earth after each day | Manure ready in 5–6 months |
| **6. Burial** | Small amounts buried in trenches and covered | Small camps, villages |

**Sanitary disposal at a rural setting**

1. **Manure pits** near the house (away from water sources).
2. **Composting** of organic waste with cattle dung and night soil.
3. **Burial** of small quantities.
4. **Biogas plants** using cattle dung and organic waste.
5. Segregation; reuse and recycling (paper, plastic, metal).
6. Health education; no throwing waste into ponds, canals or roadsides.

**Modern principles**

3R (reduce, reuse, recycle); source segregation; waste-to-energy.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.250
**Health hazards of improper disposal of solid waste**

1. **Fly breeding** → diarrhoea, dysentery, typhoid, cholera, trachoma.
2. **Rat harbourage** → plague, leptospirosis, murine typhus, rat-bite fever; food loss.
3. **Mosquito breeding** in discarded containers and tyres → **dengue**, chikungunya.
4. **Water and soil pollution** by leachate (heavy metals, pathogens, chemicals).
5. **Air pollution and odour** from decomposition and open burning (dioxins, smoke).
6. **Drain blockage** (plastics) → waterlogging, urban flooding.
7. **Injuries and infections** for waste pickers (cuts, needle-stick → HBV, HCV, HIV; tetanus).
8. Fire hazard and methane explosions at dumps.
9. Aesthetic nuisance; lower quality of life.
10. Stray animals; spread of zoonoses.

**Health hazards of improper excreta disposal**

1. **Faeco-oral diseases**: cholera, typhoid, paratyphoid, bacillary and amoebic dysentery, giardiasis, hepatitis A and E, poliomyelitis, rotavirus diarrhoea.
2. **Worm infestations**: hookworm (through soil), roundworm, whipworm, threadworm, tapeworm.
3. **Pollution** of water and soil; fly breeding; odour.

**Extent of the problem**

Diarrhoeal diseases and worm infestations remain major causes of child illness and malnutrition; one gram of faeces may carry millions of viruses and bacteria and many parasite eggs; unsafe excreta disposal is a leading environmental cause of disease, especially in slums and flood-prone areas.

**Three hazards (short answer)**

Fly-borne diarrhoeal diseases; rat-borne diseases; mosquito breeding (dengue).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.251
**Transmission of disease from excreta ("F-diagram")**

Faeces contain pathogens; they reach a new host through the "**5 Fs**":

| Route | Example |
|---|---|
| **Fluids (water)** | Contaminated wells and ponds → cholera, typhoid, hepatitis |
| **Fingers (hands)** | Unwashed hands after defecation → shigellosis, threadworm |
| **Flies** | Carry faecal matter to food → diarrhoea, typhoid |
| **Fields (soil)** | Hookworm larvae penetrate bare feet; roundworm eggs on vegetables |
| **Food** | Contaminated by the above → food poisoning, hepatitis A |

Faeces → (Fluids, Fingers, Flies, Fields) → Food / mouth → New host

**Diseases spread by excreta**

Cholera, typhoid and paratyphoid, bacillary and amoebic dysentery, giardiasis, hepatitis A and E, poliomyelitis, rotavirus diarrhoea, ascariasis, hookworm, trichuriasis, threadworm, taeniasis, schistosomiasis (urine and faeces).

**Prevention**

**Sanitation barrier** (sanitary latrine) blocks faeces at source; plus safe water, hand-washing, fly control, food hygiene, wearing footwear.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.252
**Sanitation barrier**

**Segregation of faeces** so that they cannot reach water, hands, flies, soil or food; achieved by a **sanitary latrine**. It breaks the chain of faeco-oral transmission at its **source**.

**Diagram (Park)**

```
                Water ──┐
                Hands ──┤
 Faeces ═║ BARRIER ║    Flies ──┼──→ Food → New host → Death / disease
       (sanitary   ║    Soil  ──┘
        latrine)   ║
```

Without the barrier: Faeces → water, fingers, flies, soil → food / mouth → new host.
With the barrier: faeces are isolated; all routes are blocked at once.

**How it prevents disease transmission**

1. Excreta confined in a pit or sealed system → **no access for flies** (water seal).
2. **No contamination of soil** → no hookworm larvae or worm eggs.
3. **No contamination of ground and surface water** (latrine ≥15 m from and downhill of water source).
4. Reduces **hand contamination** when combined with hand-washing.
5. Prevents **food** contamination indirectly.

**Importance in rural Bangladesh**

1. Diarrhoea, typhoid, hepatitis E and worm infestations remain common, especially after floods.
2. High water table and floods make proper latrines vital.
3. Large reductions in open defecation have occurred; maintaining and upgrading latrines (hygienic, water-sealed, flood-resilient) is the next step.
4. Low-cost **ring-slab water-seal latrines** are available.
5. Prevents child stunting linked to repeated infections.
6. Combined with safe water and hand-washing (WASH), it is the most effective prevention of faeco-oral disease.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.253
**Methods of excreta disposal (Park)**

**A. Unsewered areas**

1. **Service-type latrines (conservancy system)**: excreta removed manually by sweepers (unsanitary; being abolished).
2. **Non-service type (sanitary latrines)**:
   - (a) **Bore-hole latrine**
   - (b) **Dug-well (pit) latrine**
   - (c) **Water-seal type latrine**: PRAI type, RCA type (Bangladesh: ring-slab latrine, twin-pit pour-flush)
   - (d) **Septic tank**
   - (e) **Aqua privy**
3. **Latrines for camps and temporary use**:
   - Shallow trench latrine
   - Deep trench latrine
   - Pit latrine
   - Bore-hole latrine

**B. Sewered areas**

**Water-carriage system** → sewers → **sewage treatment** (primary: screening, grit chamber, sedimentation; secondary: trickling filter or activated sludge; sludge digestion) → safe disposal.

**Other methods**

Composting (night soil with refuse), biogas plants, ecological sanitation (urine-diverting toilets), faecal sludge management for pit and septic-tank contents.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.254
**Water-seal latrine**

Pit latrine with a squatting pan connected to a **trap holding water** (water seal ~2 cm deep) that separates the pan from the pit; flushed by pouring water. Bangladesh: **ring-slab latrine**, twin-pit pour-flush.

**Components**

Location (≥15 m from and downhill of water source) → squatting slab → pan → trap (water seal) → connecting pipe → pit (rings) → superstructure.

**Advantages in rural areas**

1. **Fly-proof**: water seal prevents flies reaching excreta → fewer fly-borne diseases.
2. **Odour-free**: seal blocks smell → people accept and use it.
3. **Prevents mosquito breeding** in the pit.
4. **Low cost**: simple materials (concrete rings, slab), locally made.
5. **Simple to build and maintain**; no daily handling of excreta; no sweeper needed.
6. **Little water needed**: 1–2 litres per flush (pour-flush).
7. **Long life** of pit; **twin-pit** design allows alternate use and safe manure.
8. Protects **ground water** and soil when properly sited.
9. **Privacy, dignity and safety** for women and girls.
10. Culturally acceptable (squatting, use of water for anal cleansing).

**Limitations**

Needs water; blocked by solid materials; pits may overflow in floods if not raised.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.255
**Criteria of a sanitary latrine (Park)**

1. Excreta should **not contaminate ground water**.
2. Excreta should **not contaminate surface soil**.
3. Excreta should **not contaminate surface water**.
4. Excreta should **not be accessible to flies or animals**.
5. **No handling of fresh excreta**; or, when unavoidable, kept to the minimum.
6. Should be **free from odours** and unsightly conditions.
7. Method should be **simple and inexpensive** in construction and operation.

**Location criteria**

- At least **15 m (50 feet)** from any water source.
- **Downhill** or on a lower level than wells.
- Pit bottom above the ground-water level where possible.
- Easily accessible, with privacy and light.

**Other desirable features**

Water seal; easy to clean; hand-washing facility with soap nearby; superstructure for privacy; raised plinth in flood-prone areas.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.256
**Sanitary landfill vs open dumping**

| Point | Open dumping | Sanitary landfill (controlled tipping) |
|---|---|---|
| Method | Waste thrown in open low-lying land | Waste spread in layers, compacted and **covered daily with earth** |
| Flies, rats, mosquitoes | Breed freely | Controlled by cover |
| Odour | Strong | Minimal |
| Open burning, fires | Common | Prevented |
| Leachate | Pollutes ground and surface water | Controlled by lining, drainage and treatment |
| Gas (methane) | Uncontrolled; fire and explosion risk | Collected or vented; can generate energy |
| Scavenging | Waste pickers exposed to injury and infection | Restricted |
| Appearance | Unsightly | Acceptable; site reclaimed later |
| Land use | Land wasted | Land reclaimed for parks, playgrounds |
| Spread of disease | High | Low |

**Advantages of sanitary landfill**

1. Hygienic; prevents vector breeding.
2. Protects water and air.
3. Relatively cheap and simple where land is available.
4. Handles all kinds of non-hazardous waste.
5. Reclaims low-lying land.
6. Landfill gas can be used as energy.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (Environment and Health).

## DU.257
**Objectives of school health services (Park)**

1. **Promotion of positive health**.
2. **Prevention of diseases**.
3. **Early diagnosis, treatment and follow-up** of defects and diseases.
4. **Awakening health consciousness** in children.
5. **Provision of a healthful school environment**.

**Aspects (components) of school health services**

1. Health appraisal of school children and school personnel.
2. Remedial measures and follow-up.
3. Prevention of communicable diseases (immunisation, deworming).
4. Healthful school environment.
5. Nutritional services (school feeding, IFA).
6. First aid and emergency care.
7. Mental health.
8. Dental health.
9. Eye health.
10. Health education.
11. Education of handicapped children.
12. Proper maintenance and use of school health records.

**Importance**

School children form a large, accessible, receptive group; healthy habits formed at school last for life and spread to families (child-to-child).

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (School Health Services).

## DU.258
**Role (priority) of school health services in rural areas**

1. **Large, accessible group**: most rural children attend primary school → easy to reach for screening, immunisation, deworming.
2. **High burden of preventable problems**: malnutrition, anaemia, worm infestations, skin and eye infections, dental caries, diarrhoea.
3. **Few health facilities** nearby → school is a key service point.
4. **Early detection** of defects (vision, hearing, learning problems) that affect schooling.
5. **Health education** reaches families through children (child-to-child, child-to-parent).
6. **Healthy habits** (hand-washing, latrine use, safe water, nutrition) are formed early.
7. **Adolescent girls**: menstrual hygiene, IFA, Td and HPV vaccination; prevention of child marriage.
8. **Improves attendance and learning**; reduces dropout.
9. Supports **national programmes**: EPI catch-up, deworming, nutrition, vaccination campaigns (MR, TCV, HPV).
10. Cost-effective, community-centred.

**Risks / challenges in rural settings**

Shortage of trained staff; poor school WASH facilities; weak follow-up and referral; poverty; lack of teacher training; poor records; low priority in budgets.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (School Health Services).

## DU.259
**Criteria of an ideal classroom (Park)**

| Item | Standard |
|---|---|
| Number of students | Not more than **40** per class |
| Floor space | At least **10 sq ft (≈1 m²) per student** |
| Ceiling / wall height | At least 10 feet |
| Windows | Window area at least **25% of floor area**; good cross-ventilation |
| Lighting | Natural light, preferably from the **left side** (for right-handed writers); no glare |
| Colour | Ceilings white; walls light colour; low reflectance for desks and floors |
| Floor | Impervious, easy to clean, non-slippery |
| Furniture | **Individual desks and chairs**; **minus-type desks**; chairs with backrest; size suited to age |
| Blackboard | Non-glare, at suitable height and distance |
| Doors | Open outward; adequate exits for emergencies |
| Noise | Away from busy roads, markets |
| Temperature | Comfortable; fans |
| Cleanliness | Clean, free from dust; dustbins |

**School environment links**

Safe drinking water; separate latrines for boys and girls (Park: 1 urinal per 60 and 1 latrine per 100 students); hand-washing facilities; playground.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (School Health Services).

## DU.260
**Health appraisal**

Assessment of the physical and mental health status of school children (and school personnel) by **periodic medical examination** and **daily observation by teachers**.

**Components**

**1. Periodic medical examination**

- At school entry, then every 4 years (Park); yearly where possible.
- **History**: past illness, immunisation, family history.
- **General examination**: anthropometry (height, weight, BMI-for-age), nutritional status, pallor, skin, hair.
- **Systemic examination**: heart (murmurs, rheumatic heart disease), lungs, abdomen.
- **Special senses**: vision (Snellen's chart), hearing (whisper test, tuning fork), dental check.
- **Investigations** as needed: Hb, stool for ova, urine.
- **Immunisation status** checked and updated.
- **Mental and behavioural** problems, learning difficulties.

**2. Daily morning inspection by the teacher**

Look for: unusual pallor, red or watery eyes, running nose, rash, cough, fever, sores, lice, dirty clothes, drowsiness, unusual behaviour; refer if needed.

**3. Health appraisal of school personnel**

Teachers and staff examined (especially for TB) because they can infect children and affect their mental health.

**4. Records**

Cumulative health record card for each child; follow-up of defects.

**Purpose**

Early detection, treatment and follow-up of defects; prevention of spread of infection; planning school health services; health education opportunity.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (School Health Services).

## DU.261
**Healthful school environment**

The physical, social and emotional conditions of the school that promote the health, safety and learning of children and staff.

**Criteria (Park)**

| Item | Criteria |
|---|---|
| **1. Location** | Central, away from busy roads, markets, factories, noise, smoke |
| **2. Site** | Elevated, well drained, fenced; enough open space |
| **3. Structure** | Safe, strong; nursery schools single-storey; walls ≥10 ft high; impervious floor |
| **4. Classroom** | ≤40 students; ≥10 sq ft per student |
| **5. Furniture** | Individual minus-type desks; chairs with backrest; suited to age |
| **6. Doors and windows** | Window area ≥25% of floor area; cross-ventilation |
| **7. Colour** | White ceilings, light-coloured walls |
| **8. Lighting** | Adequate natural light from the left |
| **9. Water supply** | Safe, adequate drinking water |
| **10. Eating facilities** | Clean place for meals; food hygiene |
| **11. Latrines and urinals** | Separate for boys and girls; 1 urinal per 60 and 1 latrine per 100 students; hand-washing with soap; menstrual hygiene facilities |
| **12. Playground** | Safe, adequate for physical activity |

**Psychosocial environment**

No corporal punishment or bullying; supportive teachers; no tobacco; inclusive of disabled children.

**How to ensure it**

School health committee; regular inspection by school health team; funds for WASH and maintenance; teacher training; student health clubs; community and parent involvement.

**Five criteria (short answer)**

Proper location and site; adequate classroom space and furniture; good lighting and ventilation; safe water and sanitary latrines; playground.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (School Health Services).

## DU.262
**School desks: classification (by horizontal distance between the edge of the seat and the edge of the desk)**

| Type | Arrangement | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| **Plus desk** | Edge of desk is **behind** (away from) the front edge of the seat → a gap | Child must bend forward to write → poor posture, fatigue, backache, spinal curvature (kyphosis, scoliosis), eye strain (book too near) |
| **Zero desk** | Edge of desk is **vertically above** the edge of the seat | Better than plus, but child still leans |
| **Minus desk** | Edge of desk **projects over** the seat | Child sits erect while writing → **best (recommended)** |

**Diagram (side view)**

```
 Plus:   desk ▁▁▁        (gap)    ▁▁▁ seat
 Zero:   desk ▁▁▁|▁▁▁ seat  (edges in line)
 Minus:  desk ▁▁▁▁▁▁           (desk overlaps seat)
              ▁▁▁▁ seat
```

**Desirable features**

1. **Minus** type; **individual** (one seat per child).
2. Height matched to child's height (adjustable or graded by class).
3. Chair with **backrest** supporting lower back; feet flat on floor.
4. Writing surface sloped (~15°) for reading; flat portion for pens.
5. Smooth, rounded edges; sturdy.

**Uses / importance**

Correct posture; prevents spinal deformities and backache; reduces eye strain and fatigue; improves attention and learning.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (School Health Services).

## DU.263
**Medical (and medicolegal) importance of the school desk**

1. **Posture**: wrong desk (plus type, wrong height) forces stooping → **kyphosis, scoliosis, lordosis**, round shoulders.
2. **Backache and neck pain**; musculoskeletal strain.
3. **Eye strain** and possible **myopia** (book held too close).
4. **Fatigue** and poor concentration → poor learning.
5. **Respiratory and digestive** discomfort from cramped chest and abdomen.
6. **Injuries** from broken desks or sharp edges.
7. **Crowding** on shared benches → spread of infections (scabies, lice, respiratory infections).

**Legal / administrative aspects**

- Schools are responsible for a **safe and healthful environment**; unsafe furniture causing injury or deformity may lead to complaints or liability.
- Furniture should follow approved **standards** (minus type, individual, size by age) during school inspection and registration.
- School health medical officers record deformities linked to furniture and recommend correction.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (School Health Services).

## DU.264
**Criteria of an ideal (school) table / desk**

1. **Minus type**: front edge projects slightly over the seat, so the child sits erect.
2. **Height** suited to the child: top at about elbow level when seated; graded by age or adjustable.
3. **Writing surface sloped** about 15° (flat strip at top for pens and inkpot).
4. **Width and depth** enough for a book and notebook (individual table).
5. **Individual** (one child per table) to avoid crowding and spread of infections.
6. **Knee room** and foot space under the table.
7. **Smooth, rounded edges**; no splinters or sharp corners.
8. **Strong, stable**, easy to clean.
9. **Non-glare, light-coloured** surface.
10. Proper **distance from eyes** (~30–35 cm) when writing.
11. Placed so that **light comes from the left**.
12. Matched with a **chair with backrest**, feet resting flat on the floor.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (School Health Services).

## DU.265
**Plus desk**

A desk in which the **front edge of the desk is behind the front edge of the seat** (positive horizontal distance) → the child must **bend forward** to write.

Effects: stooping posture, backache, kyphosis/scoliosis, eye strain, fatigue.

**Minus desk**

The **front edge of the desk projects over the seat** (negative horizontal distance).

**Importance of the minus desk**

1. Child can **sit erect** while reading and writing.
2. **Prevents spinal deformities**: kyphosis, scoliosis, round shoulders.
3. **Prevents backache** and neck strain.
4. Keeps book at proper **distance from eyes** → less eye strain and myopia risk.
5. **Less fatigue**, better concentration and learning.
6. Allows free chest expansion and comfortable breathing.
7. Recommended standard for school furniture (with individual seat and backrest).

**Comparison**

| Plus | Zero | Minus |
|---|---|---|
| Gap between desk and seat | Edges in one vertical line | Desk overlaps seat |
| Worst posture | Intermediate | **Best posture** |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (School Health Services).

## DU.266
**Common health problems of school children (Bangladesh)**

| Group | Problems |
|---|---|
| **Nutritional** | Stunting, underweight, thinness; **anaemia** (iron deficiency); vitamin A and iodine deficiency; rising overweight and obesity in urban schools |
| **Infections** | **Worm infestations** (roundworm, hookworm); diarrhoea, typhoid, hepatitis A and E; ARI, TB; dengue; measles and other vaccine-preventable diseases |
| **Skin** | Scabies, pediculosis (lice), impetigo, fungal infections |
| **Eye** | **Refractive errors** (myopia), conjunctivitis, squint, vitamin A deficiency signs |
| **Ear, nose, throat** | Otitis media, wax, hearing loss, tonsillitis |
| **Dental** | **Dental caries**, gum disease, malocclusion |
| **Heart** | Rheumatic fever and rheumatic heart disease, congenital heart disease |
| **Injuries** | **Drowning**, road traffic injuries, falls, burns, snake bite |
| **Mental and behavioural** | Learning disability, ADHD, anxiety, depression, exam stress, bullying, screen addiction |
| **Adolescent** | Menstrual hygiene problems, child marriage and early pregnancy, substance use (smoking), sexual and reproductive health issues |
| **Disability** | Visual, hearing, physical and intellectual disabilities |

**Commonest in primary schools**

Malnutrition and anaemia, worm infestation, dental caries, skin infections, eye and ear problems, diarrhoea and ARI, injuries.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (School Health Services).

## DU.267
**Role of school teachers in school health services**

1. **Daily morning inspection** of children: pallor, red eyes, rash, cough, fever, lice, scabies, unusual behaviour → refer.
2. **Health education**: hygiene, hand-washing, nutrition, dental care, safe water, road safety, menstrual hygiene; integrate into lessons.
3. **Role model** of healthy behaviour (no smoking, cleanliness, punctuality).
4. **Healthful school environment**: clean classrooms, latrines, water supply, seating, lighting.
5. **First aid** and emergency care; keep first-aid box.
6. **Assist health appraisal**: weigh and measure children, test vision (Snellen's chart), keep records.
7. **Follow-up** of children with defects; inform parents.
8. **Immunisation and campaigns**: mobilise children for vaccination (Td, HPV, MR, TCV), deworming, IFA.
9. **School feeding / nutrition** supervision.
10. **Mental health**: identify learning, emotional and behavioural problems; prevent bullying; supportive atmosphere.
11. **Inclusive education** for disabled children.
12. **Link** between school health team, parents and community; run health clubs.
13. **Exclusion and readmission** of children with communicable diseases as advised.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (School Health Services).

## DU.268
**Duties of a school health medical officer**

1. **Health appraisal**: periodic medical examination of children (at entry and at intervals) and of school staff.
2. **Treatment and referral** of minor ailments and defects; follow-up (remedial measures).
3. **Immunisation**: check and complete schedules; school vaccination campaigns.
4. **Prevention and control of communicable diseases**: surveillance, exclusion and readmission, outbreak control.
5. **Inspection of the school environment**: buildings, classrooms, furniture, lighting, water, latrines, canteen.
6. **Nutrition services**: assess nutritional status; supervise school feeding; IFA, deworming.
7. **Health education** of children, teachers and parents.
8. **Training of teachers** in daily inspection, first aid and health education.
9. **First aid and emergency care** arrangements.
10. **Mental health**: identify behavioural and learning problems; counselling; referral.
11. **Special services**: dental, eye (vision screening, spectacles), ear.
12. **Care of handicapped children**; advise on special education.
13. **Records and reports**: maintain cumulative health records; report to authorities.
14. **Coordination**: school health committee, parents, local health services.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (School Health Services).

## DU.269
**School health programmes / activities in Bangladesh**

1. **School health clinics** under DGHS in selected districts: health appraisal, treatment, referral, health education.
2. **School deworming**: periodic mass deworming of school children (albendazole/mebendazole).
3. **Immunisation in schools**: Td for adolescent girls; **HPV vaccine** for girls; school-based campaigns (measles-rubella, **typhoid conjugate vaccine**).
4. **Weekly iron-folic acid** supplementation for adolescent girls.
5. **School feeding programme** in poverty-prone areas (fortified biscuits / midday meal).
6. **WASH in schools**: safe water, separate latrines, hand-washing, menstrual hygiene management.
7. **Health education** in the national curriculum (physical education and health, life skills, adolescent health).
8. **Vision and dental screening** programmes (with NGOs).
9. **Adolescent health**: adolescent-friendly health services; school health corners; anti-child-marriage awareness.
10. **Tobacco-free and drug-free school** initiatives.
11. **Disaster preparedness** and drowning prevention (swimming lessons by NGOs).
12. **School mental health** awareness.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (School Health Services).

## DU.270
**Responsibilities of the Health Assistant (in school health and community)**

**Three main responsibilities**

1. **Immunisation**: vaccinate children and adolescent girls (Td, HPV, campaign vaccines); register and track defaulters.
2. **Health education**: personal hygiene, hand-washing, safe water, sanitation, nutrition, prevention of communicable diseases.
3. **Surveillance and reporting**: detect and report communicable diseases (measles, AFP, diarrhoea outbreaks) and refer sick children.

**Other responsibilities**

4. Help **school health appraisal**: weight, height, vision screening; record keeping.
5. **Deworming** and IFA distribution campaigns.
6. **Inspection** of school sanitation and water supply; advise on improvement.
7. **TB and leprosy** case finding; follow-up.
8. **First aid** and referral to community clinic or UHC.
9. Coordinate with teachers and school health committee.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (School Health Services; Health Care of the Community).

## DU.271
**Appliances needed by a school health medical officer**

| Purpose | Appliances |
|---|---|
| **Anthropometry** | Weighing scale, height-measuring rod (stadiometer), measuring tape, MUAC tape, growth / BMI-for-age charts |
| **General examination** | Stethoscope, BP instrument (paediatric cuffs), thermometer, torch, tongue depressors, magnifying glass |
| **Eye** | **Snellen's chart** (or E-chart), pinhole, colour vision (Ishihara) chart |
| **Ear** | Otoscope, tuning fork (512 Hz), (portable audiometer) |
| **Dental** | Mouth mirror, probe, gloves |
| **Laboratory** | Haemoglobinometer (Sahli / digital), urine dipsticks, stool containers |
| **Immunisation** (if given) | Vaccine carrier with ice packs, AD syringes, safety box, AEFI kit (adrenaline) |
| **Drugs** | Deworming tablets, IFA, ORS, paracetamol, antiseptics, skin ointments |
| **First aid** | First-aid box: bandages, gauze, splints, antiseptic, scissors, gloves |
| **Records** | Cumulative health record cards, referral slips, registers |
| **Health education** | Flip charts, posters, models, leaflets |

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (School Health Services).

## DU.272
**School health emergencies**

| Group | Emergencies |
|---|---|
| **Injuries** | Falls, fractures, sprains, cuts and bleeding, head injury, dental injuries |
| **Burns and scalds** | Laboratory, kitchen, fireworks |
| **Fainting (syncope)** | Prolonged standing at assembly, heat, hunger |
| **Medical** | Convulsions (epilepsy, febrile), **asthma attack**, anaphylaxis (insect sting, food allergy), hypoglycaemia (diabetic child), heat stroke, acute abdominal pain, high fever |
| **Bites and stings** | **Dog bite**, **snake bite**, bee sting |
| **Foreign bodies** | In eye, ear, nose; choking (airway obstruction) |
| **Nose bleed (epistaxis)** | |
| **Drowning** | Ponds near schools, during excursions |
| **Poisoning** | Food poisoning, accidental chemical ingestion |
| **Electric shock** | Faulty wiring |
| **Mass events** | Mass psychogenic illness, AEFI after school vaccination, fire, earthquake, building collapse |

**Preparedness**

Trained teachers in first aid; first-aid box; emergency contact numbers; referral to nearest health facility; safety drills.

**Ref:** Park K. Park's Textbook of Preventive and Social Medicine, 28th ed. (School Health Services).
